#3101. Deutscher Umweltpreis würdigt "Mutmacher"

Die Deutsche Bundesstiftung Umwelt zeichnet einen Ingenieur und eine Juristin aus, die Pionierarbeit für die Natur leisten. Echte Vorbilder, findet Bundespräsident Steinmeier.

Sauberes Wasser aus Kläranlagen - für diese Erfindung hat Ingenieur Klaus Vossenkaul in Aachen den Deutschen Umweltpreis der Deutschen Bundesstiftung Umwelt (DBU) erhalten. Ebenfalls ausgezeichnet wurde die als "Deutschlands wichtigste Klima-Anwältin" bekannte Juristin Roda Verheyen.

Bundespräsident Frank-Walter Steinmeier überreichte die mit insgesamt 500.000 Euro dotierte Auszeichnung. Das Preisgeld ist eines der höchsten in Europa im Bereich Umwelt. Er würdigte die beiden Preisträger als Vorbilder beim Umweltschutz. "Sie zeigen, dass jede und jeder Einzelne einen Unterschied machen kann."

Steinmeier rief bei der Verleihung in Aachen dazu auf, dem Klimaschutz trotz aller "tagespolitischen Konjunkturen" hohe Priorität einzuräumen. "Wir wissen, dass wir unsere Art zu leben, zu wirtschaften, hinterfragen müssen. Warum? Um unseren Kindern und Enkeln einen lebenswerten Planeten zu hinterlassen", sagte Steinmeier.

Der letzte Sommer habe gezeigt, welche dramatischen Folgen die Erderwärmung für alle habe: brennende Wälder, kaum noch Schifffahrt aufgrund niedriger Wasserstände und 16.000 zusätzliche Sterbefälle laut Robert Koch-Institut. "Die Fieberkurve unseres Planeten zeigt deutlich nach oben", setzte Steinmeier fort. Dabei treffe der Klimawandel Europa besonders stark - "unser Kontinent erwärmt sich mehr als doppelt so schnell wie alle anderen".

Der Deutsche Umweltpreis zeichnet Leistungen von Menschen aus, die vorbildlich zum Schutz und Erhalt der Umwelt beitragen. Der Ingenieur Klaus Vossenkaul aus der Städteregion Aachen wird für seine innovativen Weiterentwicklungen bei der Membranfiltertechnologie für die Abwasserreinigung in Kläranlagen ausgezeichnet.

Die Hamburger Rechtsanwältin Roda Verheyen bekommt den Preis für die Durchsetzung rechtlicher Vorgaben für mehr Umwelt- und Klimagerechtigkeit. Unter anderem ließ sie per Klage feststellen, dass Verursacher von Emissionen für Klimafolgeschäden haftbar sein können. 2021 gab das Bundesverfassungsgericht einer Klage Verheyens statt, wonach unzureichender Klimaschutz die Freiheitsrechte künftiger Generationen verletze.

DBU-Generalsekretär Alexander Bonde würdigte die Preisträger bei einem Festakt in Aachen als "Mutmacher, die zeigen, wie es positiv vorangehen kann". Sie verkörperten "Haltung, Beharrlichkeit und Innovationskraft mit klarem Kompass beim Umwelt- und Klimaschutz".

Steinmeier, der Anfang nächsten Jahres aus dem Amt scheidet, sagte, der Deutsche Umweltpreis sei ihm "von Jahr zu Jahr mehr ans Herz gewachsen". Er habe stets "mit Neugier und Spannung auf die Preisverleihung geschaut" und sich gefreut, "Menschen mit Engagement und innovativen Ideen kennenzulernen". Deshalb erfülle es ihn "mit ein wenig Wehmut", dass er ihn nun zum letzten Mal überreichen dürfe.

Die Nachfolgerin oder der Nachfolger von Steinmeier soll am 30. Januar von der Bundesversammlung gewählt werden. Ihr gehören die 630 Bundestagsabgeordneten und ebenso viele Wahlfrauen und -männer aus den Ländern an.

Redaktionsschluss 17.30 Uhr (MEZ). Dieser Artikel wird nicht weiter aktualisiert!

#3102. Why is the Plaza Hotel Closing? The Story Behind the Façade

Of the 700+ hotels in New York City, The Plaza Hotel stands apart. Surrounded by skyscrapers, the stout, eighteen-story building at the corner of 59th Street and 5th Avenue, just south of Central Park, is more of an icon in the cityscape than any of its super-tall neighbors could hope to be. It hosted The Beatles just before their breakthrough performance on The Ed Sullivan Show, welcomed F. Scott and Zelda Fitzgerald, inspired the eponymous Neil Simon play Plaza Suite, acted as a backdrop in Home Alone 2, and was the home of the fictional Eloise, the little girl who roamed its elegant halls. Now, nearly 120 years after it first opened, it’s shutting its doors—at least for a few years.

The Plaza's façade and the interior of the hotel are registered landmarks, recognized by both the national and city government.

Starting in 2027, the Plaza Hotel will embark on a four-year-long renovation of its marble façade. First designed by Henry Janeway Hardenbergh in 1907, the hotel’s base and lower levels are crafted from rusticated marble, with the upper levels being made from a polished, smooth marble exterior. Rusticated marble deteriorates more quickly when exposed to the elements, and can often need to be repaired and restored within twenty years. Coincidentally, the 2027 renovation comes just 19 years after the last major renovation to the hotel in 2008.

Restoring the hotel’s façade will require more than just throwing up the scaffolding and calling in a team. The façade and the interior of the hotel are recognized as registered landmarks both nationally and by the city. The Landmarks Preservation Commission is working closely with the hotel to help with the restoration efforts and preserve the architectural beauty of the building.

Interior View of the Palm Court Restaurant, 1957

“Designated as both an individual and interior landmark, the Plaza Hotel’s elegant French Renaissance-style architecture, distinctive roofline, and prominent location have made it an iconic feature of the New York City skyline,” says Courtney Metakis, from the New York Landmarks Preservation Commission. “Its grand, public interiors have made it an enduring part of the city's cultural identity for more than a century." The commission did not comment further on the plans for the renovation or guidelines to keep in line with the commission’s protected status.

While this oversight is meant to preserve the history of the hotel in the event of, say, billionaire real estate developers encroaching in the vicinity of the building, it also can make restoration projects like this vastly more complicated. The commission will require the restoration process be carefully managed to preserve the integrity of the marble and any historical details. The hotel has enlisted Allison Doyle Partners (ADP) Architects for the job—the same firm that worked on the previous renovation of the interior architecture. Design firm HLZAE will oversee the façade’s masonry restoration in particular.

Plaza Hotel, Fifth Avenue Entrance, New York, New York, 1929. The iconic hotel has been open for 120 years.

“The undertaking reflects the complexity of maintaining a property of such architectural and historical significance, with careful consideration given to preservation requirements and the building’s long-term care, as well as the safety of our guests, employees, and the wider community,” the hotel’s management said in a statement. “The scope and complexity of the façade works require detailed planning and coordination with relevant stakeholders, alongside adherence to applicable historic preservation requirements. Further considerations relating to the planning and execution of the works remain under review as the process advances.”

While the restorations are only to the marble surface of the building, the meticulous nature of the job would have disrupted all aspects of the Plaza experience for would-be guests. If you spent $1,500 for a room (or upwards) and woke up not to the sun cresting over Central Park, but because a man on a scaffold was drilling just outside your window, you would likely be upset about it.

The Plaza Hotel entrance in New York City, 1988. The last major renovation to the hotel was in 2008.

“With a building of that age, the level of architectural significance and importance to the city, requires a great deal of care and planning and execution for any restoration,” explains Frank Mahan, principal at SOM, the architecture firm that worked on a similar restoration of the historically protected façade and interior of the Waldorf-Astoria hotel in midtown over the course of ten years. “Any Landmarks building requires a certain degree of engagement with the LPC staff and sometimes at a public hearing.” Similar to the expectations of the Plaza Hotel, the Waldorf Astoria’s owners wanted to restore the façade rather than reinvent it in a new, modern image, so the LPC was involved through the process.

There’s never a good time to announce a multi-year closure, but the hotel is now working to make good on many of its long-term plans guests have made. Last April, bride-to-be Christine Carney booked the Plaza for her wedding, and was still sorting out down payments for the booking as of just a few weeks ago. Since the announcement of the closure, she's had to start looking for an alternative venue in time for the big day. The hotel is also working with union The Hotel & Gaming Trades Council to place the Plaza’s 378 staffers into new jobs for the interim of the restoration.

The real question is: Will four years be enough time? Or will the Plaza need to extend the expected closure once unforeseen needs arise, as they so often do with older buildings. “Any project with an existing building involves unexpected existing conditions,” Mahan adds. “It’s rather inevitable that once you start working on the building, you uncover things you may not have expected or that may differ from what the original drawings predicted. There’s a certain amount of flexibility and nimbleness expected on the design side.”

Luckily, the neighborhood has no shortage of historic hotels from a bygone era of the city. If you were planning a trip to stay at The Plaza (or had one booked and this is how you’re learning that it’s been canceled…) consider going just across the street to the Sherry-Netherland, up 5th Avenue another block or two to The Pierre, or around the Eastern side of Central Park for a night at the Lowell or the Carlyle.

For more of our favorite New York City hotels, see here.

#3105. Amid Drought, Hundreds of French Rivers Are Drying Up

A satellite image of low water levels along the Loire River in France on August 2. Copernicus

An ongoing drought in Europe has sapped French rivers. Officials say that 43 percent of small waterways in France have been partially or completely dried up.

Of the more than 3,000 small waterways in France, 1,373 have seen their flow interrupted or halted, according to an analysis from the French Office for Biodiversity. The agency said rivers were desiccated in every region, describing the drying as “the most critical situation ever observed at this time of year.”

Large waterways have also been hard hit. On the Loire River, water levels typically drop in the summer, inhibiting boat travel, but this year sightseeing boats had to suspend operations a month earlier than usual as the river dried up. In Paris, the Seine is being sustained by flows released from reservoirs that stored up surplus water over the winter.

Elsewhere in Europe, meager rainfall has sapped rivers, threatening the supply of water for households, farms, and power plants. Low water levels on the Danube have exposed Nazi shipwrecks and forced  the shutdown of several nuclear reactors, which draw water from the river for cooling. On the Rhine, water levels hit a record low, measuring just 10 inches deep at the German city of Kaub. Low water levels can severely hinder inland shipping.

An ongoing drought has also sapped rivers in the U.K., where officials report that nearly a quarter of rivers in England are now “exceptionally low.” Scientists say warming is helping to drive the drought, as excessive heat speeds evaporation, drying up rivers already sapped by meager rainfall.

#3107. Neighbours is being recut into a microdrama focused on Margot Robbie – and her co-stars aren’t happy

Actor’s storylines in the famous soap will be recut into vertical micro-episodes to be watched on phones – but her fellow cast members say they haven’t been consulted

The long-running Australian soap opera Neighbours is being recut with AI into a microdrama designed to be watched on phones, sparking a debate among the show’s actors and the industry over the wider implications of television archives being mined for new content.

RoseBerry Media, which specialises in making “vertical television” for phones, has repurposed original episodes of Neighbours for distribution on its Epis platform, using an AI-assisted production tool called RedSnapper.

The microdrama made from the footage will initially focus on Margot Robbie’s character, Donna Freedman, whom she played from 2008 to 2011. Two of Robbie’s storylines will be recut into vertical micro-episodes, creating about 150 minutes of content.

The Neighbours star Stefan Dennis has voiced concerns over the project, telling the Herald Sun it was “unbelievably invasive, morally and legally wrong and somehow it appears we can’t do anything about it”.

“I’ve got my agent [on it],” he said. “I imagine everyone else on Neighbours is doing the same thing, as well as the MEAA [Media, Entertainment and Arts Alliance] and Australian Writers’ Guild. Whether they can stop it is another thing. We’re dealing with a very big machine.”

Dennis’s agent told Guardian Australia he would not be commenting further.

A former Neighbours cast member, Matthew Werkmeister, who played Zeke Kinski from 2005 to 2011 and appeared alongside Robbie in a love-triangle storyline, told Guardian Australia that he only learned about the deal when he was approached for comment.

“I find it extremely concerning,” Werkmeister said. “Not from any money-making point of view, but from my image being used through AI and a platform I did not consent to.”

Werkmeister said he had received no notification from the Neighbours producer, Fremantle, or RoseBerry Media regarding whether residuals would be paid to actors in the vertical series. Guardian Australia understands that any residuals will be paid to writers and actors based on their agreements for the series, as per normal process.

“When I signed my original contracts, I was 13,” he said. “In all honestly I can’t remember exactly every line point, but AI and streaming platforms, let alone vertical streaming platforms, didn’t exist and was not a consideration.”

Werkmeister dismissed claims that the vertical format would introduce the classic soap to younger audiences and called the venture a “money grab for Fremantle”.

“They’re just trying to dump their existing library to make a quick buck,” he said. “I’d be intrigued to know if they reached out to the wonderful Margot Robbie, since her star power is likely a main motivator for this venture.”

Guardian Australia has contacted Robbie for comment.

Mattie Webb, the agent for the actor Sam Clark – who played Donna’s partner, Ringo Brown – said Clark was waiting to hear from US actors’ union Sag-Aftra before commenting.

But Webb, an LA-based manager with 27 years’ experience, said AI was rapidly disrupting the entire industry. “All week I’ve been dealing with deepfake videos and AI,” he said. “It’s a whole new world.”

The Neighbours microdrama arrives amid a boom in vertical serials, which are usually made up of episodes around just one-minute long and tailored for mobile phone viewing. The microdrama market, which originated in China, was estimated to be worth up to $15bn (A$21.4bn) globally last year.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

Some verticals are entirely original and shot for the format, while others are cut from existing material; in 2023, Paramount Pictures released Mean Girls on TikTok as 23 episodes. Others are entirely unofficial, with fans recutting their favourite shows into snippets for millions of viewers on social media.

In this era, Neighbours is a goldmine, having broadcast more than 9,300 episodes between its launch in 1985 and end in 2025. The soap also served as a launchpad for several international stars, including Robbie, Guy Pearce, Kylie Minogue, Jason Donovan, Dichen Lachman, Natalie Imbruglia, Liam Hemsworth and Jesse Spencer.

In comments reported by Variety last month, Fremantle’s commercial and international chief executive, Jens Richter, described the project as “an exciting example of how technology and creative thinking can unlock new value from our diverse catalog and content library”.

Fremantle defended the microdrama when contacted by Guardian Australia, saying that it will only use “original, previously broadcast footage, dialogue and performances”.

RedSnapper, the AI technology, “does not generate new scenes or performances, alter actors’ faces or likenesses, or use synthetic voices”, a spokesperson told Guardian Australia.

RedSnapper was used to help with “catalogue analysis, editing and vertical reframing, with a human editor reviewing and approving every stage of the editing process”.

A RoseBerry Media spokesperson said the company was aligned with Fremantle’s response and had “nothing further to add”.

Australia’s largest union for actors, the Media, Entertainment and Arts Alliance, confirmed it was assisting Neighbours cast members and their representatives after the announcement.

The MEAA acting chief executive, Adam Portelli, said anyone involved in Neighbours could contact the union “to ensure their work is not exploited without their knowledge, consent or fair compensation”.

Portelli called on the Australian prime minister, Anthony Albanese, and parliament to legislate to protect the creative sector by ensuring workers “are fairly and directly compensated when their work is exploited by AI”.

A spokesperson for the Australian Writers’ Guild told Guardian Australia it was “in discussions with Fremantle and hopes to reach a fair resolution soon”.

Werkmeister also backed legislative intervention, warning that the project would set a precedent that would reach far beyond Ramsay Street.

“Converting existing work via AI without proper consultation or fair compensation sets a concerning precedent,” he said.

“We urgently need way more robust AI regulations, guardrails and proper consent mechanisms in place.”

#3108. Retail leads the way on paternity leave as government urged to improve offer

M&S and Tesco are among employers offering six weeks, highlighting gap with construction and agriculture

“Being able to take six weeks’ paid paternity leave, flexibly, made a huge difference to me and my family, giving me the time to support my partner and settle into being a new parent,” said Marks & Spencer employee Joe Erskine.

The food and clothing retailer increased its paternity leave offering from two to six weeks at full pay in 2024, meaning Erskine benefited from the new policy when his second child was born 17 months ago.

M&S and Tesco are among the retailers leading the way in offering enhanced paternity leave to their staff, new research has shown, highlighting the widening gap between the more family-friendly policies offered to new fathers working in the retail sector compared with those in construction, manufacturing and agriculture.

Both companies offer six weeks at full pay to employees, far outstripping the statutory two-week minimum paternity leave offer and making them among the most generous of UK blue-chip companies listed on the FTSE 100, according to figures compiled by campaign group The Dad Shift and Ian Dinwiddy of Inspiring Dads.

Privately owned companies Ikea, Lego and Virgin Media O2 now also offer more weeks of fully paid leave to new fathers.

The enhanced paternity leave offered by retailers including Asda, Waitrose and Lidl in recent years means that just five employers are responsible for upgrading parental leave for nearly 650,000 workers.

Retail businesses and the shop workers’ union Usdaw held a roundtable discussion in parliament, hosted by Labour MP Connor Rand, to discuss the transformation in working parents’ lives brought in by changes to parental leave.

Rand, who was a researcher for Usdaw prior to his election to parliament in 2024, said the entitlement was crucial for his family: “Through the ups and downs, I could bond with my son and support my partner because of paternity leave. Too many dads in our country can’t do this. That’s bad for them, bad for their partner and bad for their children. It must change.”

Campaigners have long argued that the UK has one of the least generous paternity leave offers in the world, ranked 40th out of 43 Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development countries. Over two decades after paternity leave rights were introduced in Britain, they argue the country has fallen behind and now has the least generous entitlement in Europe.

Employed fathers in the UK are entitled to two weeks’ statutory paternity leave paid at £194.32 a week, or 90% of their average weekly earnings, whichever amount is lower, totalling less than half the national living wage.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Many companies offer enhanced paternity leave by topping up the statutory offer to full salary, in some cases for a longer period of time.

However, there is a growing disparity between different sectors of the economy. Analysis by The Dad Shift found that the construction sector employs around one in 10 working men, but only accounts for about one in 15 recipients of statutory paternity pay.

This could partly be because some construction workers are self-employed and currently have no entitlement to paid paternity leave, as is also the case for many of Britain’s agricultural workers, the majority of whom are self-employed.

Construction company JCB only offers two weeks’ full pay in paternity leave, although this has been increased from the three days it previously offered.

Engineering firm Rolls-Royce, which makes jet engines for planes, turbines for ships and small nuclear power plants, has a workforce that is more than 80% male but currently only offers two weeks’ fully paid leave.

More than 100 Labour MPs have called on Andy Burnham in recent weeks to commit to improving the UK’s paternity leave in the upcoming budget, warning that lowest-paid fathers are the hardest hit by inadequate paternity leave provision.

They are calling for the introduction of six weeks of paternity leave paid at 90% of earnings, capped at £1,200 a week, with the offer extended to self-employed fathers. An 18-month government review of the UK’s parental leave system is expected to report later this year.

#3110. Stichwahl in Brasilien: Kopf-an-Kopf-Rennen zwischen Lula und Bolsonaro

Feiert Bolsonaro ein Comeback? Flavio Bolsonaro, Sohn des Ex-Präsidenten, geht als Favorit in die Stichwahl gegen Amtsinhaber Lula. Umfragen sagen ein Kopf-an-Kopf Rennen voraus. Und eine Zerreißprobe für das Land.

Das zehntgrößte Industrieland Brasilien geht an diesem Sonntag erneut an die Wahlurnen. In der Stichwahl stehen sich der langjährige Präsident Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva von der Arbeiterpartei (PT) und der rechtspopulistische Senator Flávio Bolsonaro (PL), Sohn des Ex-Präsidenten Jair Bolsonaro, gegenüber.

Umfragen deuten darauf hin, dass die Wahl äußerst knapp ausfallen dürfte. Beide Kandidaten liegen regelmäßig innerhalb der statistischen Fehlermarge gleichauf, was auf ein schwer vorhersehbares Ergebnis hindeutet.

In einer am Samstag veröffentlichten Umfrage des Meinungsforschungsinstituts Datafolha kam Flávio auf 51 Prozent der gültigen Stimmen, gegenüber 49 Prozent für Lula. Angesichts einer Fehlermarge von zwei Prozentpunkten gilt dieses Ergebnis jedoch als statistisches Unentschieden.

Eine am selben Tag veröffentlichte Nexus-Umfrage sah Flávio bei 50,5 Prozent und Lula bei 49,5 Prozent. Auch hier liegt der Abstand innerhalb der Fehlermarge von 2,2 Prozentpunkten.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Im ersten Wahlgang am 4. Oktober hatte Flávio 47,03 Prozent der Stimmen erreicht, während Lula lediglich auf 45,16 Prozent kam. Der Abstand betrug damit lediglich 1,87 Prozentpunkte.

Neben den gleichen Nachnamen wie bei der letzten Wahl bringt die Stichwahl erneut eine entscheidende Auseinandersetzung zwischen zwei politischen Projekten mit sich, die gegensätzlicher kaum sein könnten.

Auf der einen Seite setzt Lula erneut auf die Erinnerung an sozialpolitische Maßnahmen, die während seiner früheren Regierungen (2003 bis 2011) eingeführt wurden, ergänzt durch Appelle zur Verteidigung der nationalen Souveränität.

Flávio hingegen setzt auf Revanche und will seine politische Dynastie nach der Niederlage von 2022 wieder an die Macht bringen. Ähnlich wie sein Vater verfolgt er eine Strategie, die auf die Ablehnung der PT in Teilen der brasilianischen Gesellschaft baut.

Zudem setzt er auf eine Konfrontationskurs gegenüber Institutionen wie dem Obersten Bundesgericht (STF) und greift Themen wie die steigenden Lebenshaltungskosten auf.

Beide Wahlkämpfe standen zudem im Schatten der ethischen und institutionellen Krise der Justiz, des Aufflammens von Korruptionsskandalen, geopolitischer Spannungen und sogar von Vorwürfen ausländischer Einflussnahme, sowohl durch die US-Regierung als auch großer US-Technologiekonzerne.

Das Wahlergebnis wird auch die Rolle beeinflussen, die Brasilien in der Welt einnimmt. Ein möglicher Sieg von Lula im größten Land Südamerikas würde sowohl in Europa, dessen Staaten sich nach der Ära Jair Bolsonaro wieder Brasilien angenähert haben, als auch bei Verfechtern des Multilateralismus und von Umweltanliegen mit Erleichterung aufgenommen werden.

Ein Sieg Flávio Bolsonaros würde nicht nur bei Umweltschützern Besorgnis auslösen, sondern Brasilien voraussichtlich auch stärker an den USA unter Donald Trump ausrichten. Damit würde das Land einem Trend folgen, der bereits in anderen Staaten der Region zu beobachten ist, etwa in Argentinien unter Javier Milei und im Kolumbien von Abelardo de la Espriella.

Brasiliens Stichwahl findet nur wenige Tage vor dem siebten Jahrestag von Lulas Freilassung aus dem Hauptquartier der Bundespolizei in Curitiba statt. Der Präsident hatte dort 580 Tage in Haft verbracht.

Nach seiner Freilassung vollzog Lula nicht einmal drei Jahre später eine der spektakulärsten politischen Wendungen der brasilianischen Geschichte, als er 2022 den damaligen Präsidenten Jair Bolsonaro besiegte und eine dritte Amtszeit gewann.

Während seiner dritten Amtszeit seit 2023 zeigten zahlreiche wirtschaftliche Kennzahlen Brasiliens nach dem Rückgang am Ende der Amtszeit von Jair Bolsonaro Anzeichen einer Verbesserung.

Das Land erreichte Rekordwerte bei der Beschäftigung sowie die niedrigsten Armuts- und Extremarmutsraten seit Beginn der Datenerhebung im Jahr 2012. Diesen Verbesserungen standen allerdings stark gestiegenen Lebenshaltungskosten gegenüber.

Da Lula sich inzwischen seinem 81. Geburtstag nähert, könnte dies die letzte Präsidentschaftswahl sein, bei der sein Name auf dem Stimmzettel steht. Im Wahlkampf 2026 setzte er erneut auf sein politisches Erbe und seine persönliche Lebensgeschichte.

Obwohl er in den Umfragen zunächst vorne lag, erwies sich der erste Wahlgang für den PT-Politiker als schwieriger als erwartet. Im Vergleich zur ersten Runde der Wahl von 2022 verlor Lula rund 3,4 Millionen Stimmen.

Lulas Wahlkampfteam reagierte nach der Enttäuschung über das Ergebnis der ersten Runde mit einer umfassenden Mobilisierung ihrer Anhängerschaft und einem verstärkten digitalen Auftritt, um den Rückstand noch aufzuholen.

Flávio hingegen geht, von einer Rechtswelle getragen, die im ersten Wahlgang den Nationalkongress erfasste, als Favorit in die Stichwahl.

Der 45-Jährige versprach, seinem Vater Jair Bolsonaro, der wegen eines versuchten Staatsstreichs verurteilt und inhaftiertwurde, "Gerechtigkeit widerfahren zu lassen". Er sprach sich mehrfach für eine Amnestie aller Beteiligten an den Angriffen am 8. Januar 2023 aus.

Daneben konzentrierte Flávio seinen Wahlkampf auf die Kritik an den steigenden Lebenshaltungskosten, die Themen Korruption und Drogen. Er versprach einen "Krieg" gegen das, was er als "Narkoterrorismus" bezeichnete, und orientierte sich damit an anderen Vertretern der lateinamerikanischen extremen Rechten, wie etwa dem salvadorianischen Präsidenten Nayib Bukele.

In der letzten Debatte vor den Wahlen beim TV-Sender Globo erklärte Flávio: "Lula hat Rindersteaks versprochen und Kürbis geliefert".

Lula hingegen fasste zusammen, was aus seiner Sicht auf dem Spiel steht: "Entweder wir halten die Demokratie am Leben, oder wir werden einen Milizionär haben, der dieses Land regiert. Das ist für niemanden gut. Machen Sie dieses Experiment nicht", warnte er.

Da Umfragen zeigen, dass die Wählerschaft beider Kandidaten weitgehend festgelegt ist . So geben bei Flávio Bolsonaro beispielsweise 94 Prozent seiner Anhänger an, ihre Wahlentscheidung nicht mehr ändern zu wollen.

Lulas größte Herausforderung besteht in der Stichwahl daher darin, die kleine Gruppe der Unentschlossenen noch für sich zu gewinnen und jene Wähler zu mobilisieren, die im ersten Wahlgang nicht abgestimmt haben.

Sollte Flávio gewählt werden, würde er als Präsident über einen stärkeren politischen Rückhalt verfügen als einst sein Vater im Jahr 2018. Denn im brasilianischen Kongress könnte er mit der Unterstützung von einer Rekordzahl an neu gewählten Abgeordneten und Senatoren seiner Partei PL rechnen.

#3111. Switch To 24-Hour Clock Time ASAP—Because With Travel, Sometimes Time Really Is Money

There I was, nice and early for my 3pm bus from Bratislava, Slovakia to Vienna, Austria. It was 2pm and I wasnât seeing on any of the monitors my busâ details, so I found someone to ask.

Thatâs when I was told that my bus had left,Â on time,Â about 12 hours earlier at 3amâor 03:00 as it showed on my ticket.Â

NotÂ 3pm, or what would have been 15:00 had I purchased for the time I thought I was. Thatâs the moment I decided to switch my brain…by switching my digital clocks…to 24-hour clock format vs. 12-hour clock format.Â

Did I mention this was a trip when I was 41 years old and I was only seeing my 4th country outside the US? Yeah, international travel was still extremely new and scary…and fraught with potentially costly mistakes!

This site participates in the Amazon Services LLC Associates Program and other affiliate programs and may earn from qualifying purchases.

Lucky for me, there were busses traveling literally every hour back and forth between those two destinations that day. My rookie travel mistake only cost me another $10 ticket and a sliver of pride. But things could have been much more expensive and time-sensitive had it been for a high-speed, cross-country train or the like.

Thatâs one of the biggest reasons Iâm going to encourage you to make the change to using 24-hour clock time ASAPâlike right now! Time really can mean big money if you make a mistake that starts a domino effect of other costly missed connections. If you plan to travel internationally, especially solo, there will be plenty of other pitfalls to avoid.

Especially if youâre not a seasoned international traveler, this is just one more tiny thing that can give you anxiety and leave room for errors. The sooner you switch from using 12-hour clock time to 24-hour clock time, the sooner it will become second nature.

And yes, the 24-hour clock is sometimes referred to as military time, especially in the US. But because it’s just kind of the “normal” way you talk time in most countries, there’s really no reason to give it a name like “military time” when you’re talking time around the world!

READ NEXT: The Learn To Travel Series: A Brief Apology To FearUPDATED: 18 Safe Traveling Tips For NoobsâKeeping Safe & Reducing Travel Anxiety

Want to travel more but donât really know where to start? Have a friend or family member that keeps talking about travelâbut clearly needs a little help actually getting off go and making it happen? This is the no-fluff, hands-on guide to finally getting unstuck and on a travel pathâwith confidence, intention, and zero shame.

Oddly enough, thereâs not really a big change to your daily habits with a change to using 24-hour clock time. Back before I switched from a 12-hour clock to a 24-hour clock I thought it was going to be really complicated and break my brain. In reality, learning to use a 24-hour clock has only made me feel more confident as it has become almost second nature to look at a 24-hour clock time and know what the numbers mean.

Rest assured that people still talk the language youâve always known. 5am, 7pm, 11pm…they all still translate in speech and text messages. Very few people really use 19:00 (for 7pm), or 23:00 (for 11pm), etc., other than when discussing time tables or when in extremely specific professionsâlike doctors or those in the military. In professions like those, making sure there is absolutely no mistaking the time is paramount.

And phrases like “in the morning,” or “at night” will always still be used to help clarify.

Otherwise, the everyday uses of time in conversation are really going to look and act almost exactly the same as youâve known your whole life! For all of these reasons, I see very little downside to the change!

First things first, with any time beyond noon (12pm), the written hours simply keep going up by 1 number until the day/clock resets at midnight. As you learn to use 24-hour clock time, this simple tip will help you with understanding the afternoon and evening hours.

Simply subtract 12 from any time “greater” than 12:00 to get the verbal “pm” equivalent.

For example, if the 24-hour clock says 20:00, subtract 12 from 20, which leaves you with 8…so 20:00 is 8pm! If the 24-hour clock says 16:30, subtract 12 from 16, which leaves 4…then add the :30 and you have 4:30pm!

Yes, whole number times (on the hour) are easier, but 24-hour clock times with minutes included become second nature very quickly once you use the rule of simply subtracting 12 from any time greater than 12:00.

Keep in mind that morning hours (before noon) with a “0” (zero) in front are read like: “O-100” for 1am or “O-300” for 3am, typically said like the letter O, as opposed to literally saying “zero.” Then afternoon hours are said with hundreds, like “fourteen hundred” for 14:00/2pm, or “twenty-one hundred” for 21:00/9pm. And yes, it would literally be “twenty-hundred” for 20:00/8pm, although I’ve never heard anyone actually say that in practice!

READ NEXT: Travel Medical Insurance, Pre-Existing Conditions, & Type 1 DiabetesAnalysis Paralysis? Rigid Plans Are OverratedâBackUp Plans For The Win!

Coming from the US, and having not traveled internationally until later in life, I was under the assumption that using 12-hour clock time was the norm. Well, Iâm sure you know what they say about assumptions. Now that Iâm a little more versed in international travel, and having done the research, Iâve realized that my assumptions couldnât have been much further from the truth.

In reality, there are only about 18 countries around the world that use 12-hour clock time vs. 24-hour clock time.

And in case youâre as bad at geography or as new to world travel as I am…there are 196 countries in the world, depending on who’s classifying them. That means that barely 10% of the countries in the world use a 12-hour clock.

If youâre using a smartphone, like over 80% of US adults, youâll likely just need to go to the settings of your clock app and switch to 24-hour clock time. It may be as easy as just searching in your settings for âclockâ to find this. Otherwise, just do a quick google search for your model of phone, maybe your operating system, and the phrase âswitch to 24-hour clockâ and you should quickly find it.

Jason is the author of “The Beginner Traveler’s Guide” as well as the voice behind the words and the eye behind the lens for The Nomad Experiment. “Planning to travel at some point” wasn’t actually getting the job done, so nearing 40 he decided to make it a priority, nomatter how scary that was. A few years laterâthrough the pandemic and a type 1 diabetes diagnosis at age 42ânow living a life of nomadic travel, he’s speaking out to encourage others of any age, or with any serious medical diagnosis, to live an unconventional life.

Figure out where you are going & how are you getting there…I suggest using at least 2 to 3 different travel search sites. Start with Skyscanner or Orbitz or Booking …or whatever aggregator site you prefer. Then when you see what airlines to use, check their respective sites for better deals or rewards flights.

Figure out where you’re going to stay…If you’re interested in hostels, search Hostelworld or Hostelling International. For longer-term or more private digs, look at Airbnb, VRBO, colivings, or you can look for hotel rooms in the links from the search engines listed above.

Get comprehensive travel insurance, or in the least, travel medical insurance if internationally…Especially with COVID not going anywhere, you need travel medical insurance nomatter what! Start with an insurance aggregator or with SafetyWing,Â World Nomads, or another. Then decide what is important to you; trip cancellation, baggage coverage, medical, or all of the above. And get a yearly evacuation plan, since you’ll have to get home after your emergency!Â

#3114. Robert Williamson obituary: geneticist who mapped genes for muscular dystrophy and cystic fibrosis

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Kay E. Davies is Dr Lee’s Professor of Anatomy emeritus at the University of Oxford, Oxford, UK. She was a research fellow with Williamson at St Mary’s Hospital.

Credit: Prudence Cuming Associates/The Royal Society

Robert (Bob) Williamson was a geneticist who dedicated his career to the identification of disease-linked genes and the diagnosis and treatment of single-gene disorders. He was concerned about the ethical implications of population-wide genetic screening and using stem cells in research. Williamson was one of the first to use DNA markers to locate the position of important human disease-associated genes on chromosomes. He mapped the location of genes linked to cystic fibrosis (CF) and Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD). This led to the development of genetic tests to identify people who carried DMD-related gene alterations, extending the range of counselling provided to affected families.

He was an astute politician, bringing together people from across the field of health care — from genetic research to the bedside — and working closely with patient advocacy groups and the UK government. After moving from the United Kingdom to Australia, he became interested in national science policy and medical and scientific ethics. There, he advised several premiers and ministers.

All the ways a cell can die — and why the variety matters

All the ways a cell can die — and why the variety matters

Williamson was born in Cleveland, Ohio, and spent his childhood in New York City. When he was 16 years old, his family moved to London. After doing undergraduate and graduate degrees in chemistry at University College London, Williamson moved to the University of Glasgow, UK, in 1963 to become a lecturer in developmental biology. There, his research focused on globin genes, which encode the protein components of the oxygen-carrying haemoglobin molecule. His group showed that blood disorders known as thalassaemias were often caused by deletions in the genes encoding α-globin and fetal haemoglobin. He later collaborated with others to develop prenatal diagnosis and testing methods for carriers of these disorders.

His work on haemoglobin synthesis in immature red blood cells led him to study the control of gene expression. Puzzled about unexpected free-floating DNA fragments that hindered his work, he reported in 1970 that this cell-free DNA was released by the nuclei of cells that had died during development (R. Williamson J. Mol. Biol. 51, 157–160; 1970). In the same paper, he described the ‘nucleosome ladder’, a gel-electrophoresis pattern that reflected how DNA was organized in chromosomes. Williamson was particularly proud that these observations were recognized; his paper was published three years before the biological importance of nucleosomes was realized and several decades before cell-free DNA became a valuable diagnostic tool for cancer and other conditions.

CRISPR’s next act: the companies editing the epigenome to treat disease

#3115. As Deforestation Falls in Brazil, It Surges in Neighboring Bolivia

A satellite image of forest cleared to grow sugarcane in Bolivia. Planet Labs

As Brazil cracks down on the illegal clearing of forests, farmers and ranchers are moving into Bolivia, helping to fuel a wave of deforestation in a country where land is cheap and enforcement lax.

As Bolivia faces high inflation, waning revenues from natural gas, and widespread unrest, the government is looking to soybeans and other commodity crops to drive growth, Bloomberg reports. A recent influx of growers from Brazil and other countries has expanded the agricultural footprint, at the expense of native forests.

In Bolivia, the acreage of original vegetation lost yearly has nearly doubled, increasing from less than 900,000 acres in 2022 to nearly 1.8 million acres in 2024, a record high, according to data from MapBiomas.

While Brazil has stepped up monitoring and enforcement, Bolivia is still “in the first stages” of forming conservation policy, Stasiek Czaplicki, a Bolivian economist, told Bloomberg. Bolivia has imposed few constraints on the clearing of forest. According to one estimate, the production of one ton of soy in Bolivia is associated with seven times more deforestation than the same amount in Brazil.

In Brazil, deforestation in 2026 is on track to hit its lowest level since 1988. The sharp decline comes amid a push to stem the clearing of forest by miners, loggers, and farmers under President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, who returned to office in 2023. Brazil is aiming for net zero deforestation by the end of this decade.

#3116. ‘Synthetic twin’: Why Socceroo Cristian Volpato is the latest footballer to license his AI clone

Athletes’ scramble to control – and monetise – their digital identities at the cutting edge of AI rights deals

Get our new political email, free app or daily news podcast

The Socceroos star stares at the camera as he strides through an enormous atrium, the sound of his football boots on polished concrete echoing around him. Suddenly, he spawns four digital clones that peel off out of view to re-emerge in different settings around the world.

This scene from a social media video is fake and generated by artificial intelligence. But it has been authorised by the very real 22-year-old footballer, Cristian Volpato, the latest professional athlete to licence and monetise his AI likeness.

Draped in a white linen suit with his feet on the sand, Volpato’s “synthetic twin” turns towards a photographer as the limestone stacks of the Twelve Apostles tower behind him. Another dominates a large digital billboard wearing a football jersey. One smiles into a mirror while adjusting a sharp grey suit.

This video was produced by a new company that acts as an AI licensing agent for professional athletes. Experts believe it is one small part of a nascent online industry that could transform the future of image rights and sports content.

As bookmakers, broadcasters and brands increasingly use athletes’ images in AI generated content without their permission, some players are fighting back.

In mid-September, Volpato, who plays for Italian Serie A club, Sassuolo, struck a deal with the California-based company Callandor to license his AI image.

As part of the deal, Callandor was given exclusive rights to Volpato’s digital likeness, voice, biometric profiles and performance data. The company will monetise his AI image by landing brand deals, taking a cut, and help restrict content that does not have his approval.

From Los Angeles, Callandor’s co-founder, Michael Fisk, tells Guardian Australia he’s in the business of making “synthetic twins”.

“Cristian is constantly training,” Fisk says. “He’s constantly out there playing. If he has any free time whatsoever, he’ll probably want to spend it with family and friends, so he loses on a lot of opportunities on brand placements.

“A synthetic twin could be used 24-7, year-round by brands. Assuming it’s approved, right?”

Founded in February, Callandor has a small stable of athletes, including Brazilian footballer Jorginho and American sailor Chris Poole. Its campaigns are only in the pilot phase, but Callandor is pursuing an array of opportunities beyond traditional advertising.

Fisk, a former executive at Sony Pictures and MGM, talks of creating AI-generated book voiceovers with foreign language editions. His team is also plotting apps that offer fans a personal relationship with a synthetic twin.

“Ultimately, the holy grail of an athlete is to have that one-on-one interaction with fans,” Fisk says.

He raises the prospect of creating an app that monitors how people play football. A digital clone of Volpato would give real-time instructions.

“He’s realising I’m not handling the ball well [so] his synthetic twin can tell me, ‘Hey, you should be doing this and this and this’,” says Fisk.

“Volpato would never be able to do that one-on-one with [them].”

Volpato’s deal with Callandor is at the cutting edge of AI rights deals, but he isn’t the first to embrace the technology. In June, Brazilian superstar Neymar licensed his AI likeness to a Chinese-owned entertainment platform that created short videos published during the FIFA World Cup.

These projects are being launched as governments across the world consider basic regulation of AI. At the moment, player contracts with major Australian sporting leagues make no mention of AI.

But technology isn’t waiting for regulation to catch up and Fisk says his synthetic twin clients are blurring the lines between real and fake.

“It’s becoming almost impossible to detect, that look and feel, the difference, particularly when you see it on a mobile device,” he says.

William Saunders leads a small Sydney based start-up, Hyperknown, that’s trying to cut similar deals. He says athletes are beginning to realise that if they don’t monetise their AI image, others will.

Earlier this month, the AFL told its gambling partner Sportsbet to stop using AI images of players without their consent. This was prompted by a complaint from the players’ union, which is considering whether stricter rules should be negotiated.

“Sportsbet shows us what happens when companies and technology get ahead of consent,” Saunders says.

“Volpato represents the counter-move: people establishing their rights before others start making decisions about their identities.

“We’re watching a race between what technology can do and what people have actually authorised.”

Many in the Australian sports industry, including content creators, bookmakers, broadcasters and athletes themselves, have started embracing the technology.

Earlier this month, Australian cricketer Travis Head personally appeared in an ad for the broadcaster Kayo. He joked that his AI clone had already finished filming the ad, before throwing to vision of him riding a flying kookaburra.

Brand deals and endorsements have become major money makers for athletes, with some securing multimillion dollar contracts. It remains unclear whether brands will be willing to pay as much for an AI clone, as opposed to the real deal.

But Saunders, whose company has only just launched, says the opportunities are enormous. He talks of AI clones selling products in foreign languages in nations athletes have never visited.

“The next trust signal won’t just say ‘made with AI’,” he says, referencing a tag often used by social media companies. “It will say ‘made with this person’s permission’.

“AI can make content abundant. It can’t manufacture someone’s permission. That’s becoming a new commercial requirement.”

Jonathan Harlen, an expert in intellectual property law at Southern Cross University, says the rise of AI brokers is “the obvious next step” in a “supercharged global market”.

From a legal perspective, he says this new industry presents “a tangle of different interests and rights” that need to be carefully negotiated.

“While copyright already subsists in voice recordings, artistic works and cinematographic images, it is only recognised where there has been human authorship, as well as a degree of independent or creative human effort involved in the making of the work,” Harlen says.

Celebrities and athletes do have some protection against AI-generated content using their image and voice without consent. But these “passing off” laws only apply when public figures can prove they have suffered an economic loss.

This has not slowed an explosion of deepfakes generated by increasingly sophisticated off-the-shelf software.

In the UK last month, a campaign was launched by some the world’s top footballers, including Cole Palmer and Lucy Bronze, which highlighted their concerns about deepfakes and the inability for regulation to slow their explosion. This campaign was backed by the global player union, Fifpro.

One offshore casino used a deepfake video of Manchester United player Bruno Fernandes in advertising earlier this year.

Not all unauthorised deepfakes will be unwanted, however. Fisk says a heavy-handed crackdown on “fan art” using AI tools might be counterproductive.

“You don’t want fans to use your image and put you in [content] using brands that you don’t use, or saying something that you would never say,” he says.

“Obviously, anything with religion and drugs, or whatever it might be, you have to be very, very careful. So it does complicate things a bit, but the opportunities are phenomenal.”

But in this emerging realm, the consequences may be hard to predict.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3117. Cutting ‘red tape’ is nothing but a red herring – and arguably counterproductive | Heather Stewart

Blaming a moribund economy on too many rules is a hackneyed neoliberal trope – UK plc needs smarter regulation, not less regulation

Before becoming prime minister, Andy Burnham pledged to “end 40 years of neoliberalism”. But as he prepares for his first budget on Wednesday, his government is yet to let go of one of its most tedious cliches: red tape.

In the run-up to the chancellor John Healey’s high-stakes Commons address, the government issued a press release last week promising his plans would include measures to “slash the red tape choking firms”.

Named after the tape once used to tie up government bills, and popularised by the historian Thomas Carlyle, the phrase is straight from the neoliberal playbook and has long outlived its usefulness.

Getting the interfering state out of the way of the wealth creators was a trope of Thatcher and Reaganite economics, and on this side of the pond it has been repeated ad nauseam by every government since.

Thatcher unleashed the City with her “big bang” deregulation drive, and her successor John Major was enthusiastic about “hacking back the jungle of red tape”. It was the man he appointed for the job – Michael Heseltine – who coined what has become the most hackneyed description of this particular approach: the “bonfire of red tape”.

With Labour back in power, Gordon Brown celebrated a light-tough regulatory regime for the UK’s hard-driving financial sector – until the Treasury had to bail out the banks after the 2008 financial crisis.

Boris Johnson wanted to unfetter Britain’s businesses from “Brussels bureaucracy” and waffled on during the Brexit campaign about bendy bananas. Yet, after Brexit, business groups said their members would rather comply with longstanding EU standards in many areas than adopt an entirely new UK regime. And bananas are no more or less bendy than before.

Rachel Reeves had a more unpleasantly graphic way of making a similar point, referring to regulation as a “boot on the neck” of British business.

There is nothing wrong with ensuring the interaction between business and the state is smooth. But even during the early years of neoliberalism, the free market good, regulation bad dichotomy was always unhelpful, for several reasons.

First, good regulation – a robust competition regime, for example – is essential to make markets work well. And even most enthusiasts for a laissez-faire approach would acknowledge the need for some basic quality standards – for food and medicines, for example.

When it comes to the labour market, Labour has unashamedly adopted a pro-regulation stance. Its Employment Rights Act is the most significant increase in workers’ rights in a generation – but it inevitably, and deliberately, increases the rules firms must follow.

That is to the benefit, ministers rightly argue, of low-paid employees trapped in insecure work. But a bonfire of red tape it is not. Labour posing as a champion of deregulation will seem unconvincing, given that context.

Second, and ironically, the march of neoliberalism itself is a reason regulation has mushroomed in recent decades. As the Labour MP Yuan Yang argued in a recent lecture for the journal Renewal, as the state retreated from providing important services, watchdog institutions to oversee the private sector became necessary.

As she put it: “The neoliberal illusion, that privatisation liberated markets from state interference, obscures the reality that the state already significantly controls our privatised utilities, but through a maze of arm’s length regulatory agencies which operate outside democratic influence.”

For the water sector, privatised in 1989, these include Ofwat, which sets bills (with the Competition and Markets Authority acting as an appeals body in the event of a dispute); the Drinking Water Inspectorate; the Environment Agency; and the Consumer Council for Water.

Yet, as Yang points out, none are responsible for drawing up a coherent plan for the sector as a whole. “Everyone knows how this story ends: with our rivers polluted and our water infrastructure dangerously lacking.”

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Hacking away at the regulatory thicket fails to deal with the structural features of the economy that make the rules necessary in the first place.

Mat Lawrence, the director of the thinktank Common Wealth and author of The Productive State, a policy paper influential among those close to Burnham, calls this “the regulatory state”. He cites affordable housing targets for private developers as another example of regulation taking the place of stuff the state once did itself.

The third reason the endless red tape discourse is unhelpful is one raised by Mike Clancy, the general secretary of the Prospect union, which represents workers at many of the regulators.

He has urged the government to lay off these often not lavishly paid staff, who are trying to do their jobs with stretched resources. “Regulators continue to be labelled as ‘blockers’ despite the vital work they do keeping us safe and protecting our natural environment,” he said.

Prospect has been urging the government to ditch a promise to “cut the administrative burden” of regulation by 25% by the end of the parliament, arguing it is arbitrary and potentially counterproductive.

Finally, we would all benefit from some over-mighty businesses being reined in by regulation, not least the tech firms, which insouciantly concede that their AI agents have been busting through safety controls and may be about to start spawning spontaneously.

Embracing the tired idea that red tape is “choking” business is hardly likely to help the government make the case for the more stringent approach that is clearly necessary.

As it happens, Bonfire Night is almost upon us. If Burnham really wants to embrace a different approach to the economy, he could start by consigning the phrase “red tape” to the flames.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3118. How to Make Light-Up DIY Christmas Cards with Paper Circuits

These light up paper circuit cards were much easier to make than I expected and only need a few inexpensive materials.

They’re a great way to learn about circuits and electricity. You can make a simple, basic circuit or a more complicated series or parallel circuit. I’ve created a free template to make the activity as easy as possible.

Electric current is the flow of charge around a circuit, it can only flow if the circuit is complete. This means that if there’s a gap in the circuit the LED won’t light up.

A battery acts like a pump, pushing the electric charge around the circuit.

Use my free card templates or draw your own!

Adult supervision is needed with this activity.

Either print out one of my templates or draw a picture on the outside of a folded piece of A4 card that you can insert a coloured LED into.

Look at the LED, the long leg is postitive + and the short leg is negative -.

The top of the battery is positive and the bottom is negative.

A simple circuit has one battery, one LED and a complete square made with copper tape.

We left a gap in the circuit that is only closed wheh the card is closed. This means the LEDs light up only when the card is closed and the circuit is complete.

When making the cicruit the copper tape attached to the long leg of the LED should lead into the positive side of the battery.

Check the circuit, try flipping the battery or the LED.

Press down on the copper tape to make sure there are no looose connnections.

Make sure the copper tape isn’t touching itself by the LED legs or the battery as this can cause a short circuit.

Once you’ve mastered a basic circuit, try a parallel circuit with 2 LEDs.

If you enjoyed the activity, don’t forget to check out my other easy electricity projects.

#3120. Audi kündigt Milliarden-Investition in den USA an

Die Volkswagen-Tochter Audi baut ein eigenes Werk in den USA. Angekündigt wurde das auf einer Wahlkampfveranstaltung der Republikaner von US-Präsident Donald Trump. Noch sind allerdings Fragen offen.

Der amerikanische Präsident Donald Trump sagte in der Stadt Perry im US-Bundesstaat Georgia, Audi investiere vier Milliarden Dollar in das Werk. Jährlich sollten dort rund 200.000 Fahrzeuge vom Band laufen.

Audi-Chef Gernot Döllner bestätigte die Ausführungen Trumps und erklärte: "Wir planen, unsere größten und hochwertigsten SUVs hier in Macon, Georgia, zu produzieren". Er ergänzte: "Dies ⁠ist ⁠eine langfristige Investition, weil wir an den amerikanischen Markt glauben."

Die Ankündigung des deutschen Autobauers spielt dem amerikanischen Präsidenten in die Hände. Am 3. November stehen in den USA Zwischenwahlen an, und seine Republikaner drohen, die Mehrheit im Kongress zu verlieren. In Georgia wird zusätzlich auch der Gouverneur neu gewählt. Trump will deutlich machen, dass seine Zollpolitik der US-Wirtschaft hilft. ​Der republikanische US-Senator Bernie Moreno sagte, das Audi-Werk wäre "ohne eine wirtschaftsfreundliche Regulierung niemals zustande gekommen".

Kritiker führen dagegen an, dass die ​von der Trump-Regierung auferlegten Zölle Autos verteuert und Investitionen verzögert haben. Audi importiert derzeit die Fahrzeuge für den US-Markt aus ​Europa und Mexiko. Ein ‌eigenes US-Werk steht seit mehr als einem Jahrzehnt auf der Wunschliste der Ingolstädter.

Audi zufolge sollen durch die Investition rund 4500 Arbeitsplätze entstehen. Der Baubeginn für das Werk in Macon sei für das kommende Jahr geplant, die Produktion solle Anfang der 2030er-Jahre anlaufen. Jährlich sollen rund 200.000 Fahrzeuge gebaut ‌werden. In dem Werk der Volkswagen-Tochter sollen die Modelle Audi Q7, Q8 und Q9 montiert werden, sagte ein US-Regierungsvertreter der Nachrichtenagentur Reuters. Zudem plane ‌Audi, ​jährlich 25.000 Fahrzeuge im benachbarten South Carolina zu bauen, auch für den Export.

Volkswagen-Chef Oliver Blume sagte, Nordamerika sei ein fester Bestandteil des Zukunftsplans. Die Region sei ein wichtiger Markt, um profitabel zu wachsen. "Ein mögliches Werk für Audi in den USA würde dafür weitere Voraussetzungen schaffen: Lokalisierte Produkte aus den USA für die USA." Derzeit spielt Audi auf dem US-Markt kaum eine Rolle. Die von Trump eingeführten Zölle hatten den Autobauer im vergangenen Jahr mit 1,2 Milliarden Euro belastet. Der Absatz von Audi ging in den USA in diesem Jahr bislang um 16 Prozent zurück.

Ungeachtet der von Präsident Trump und Audi-Chef Döllner gemachten Erklärungen zum Bau eines eigenen Audi-Werkes in den USA steht die Zustimmung des Volkswagen-Aufsichtsrats noch aus, und dort dürfte die Ankündigung für ⁠Gesprächsstoff sorgen. ​Niedersachsens Ministerpräsident Olaf Lies sagte, es sei strategisch richtig, den US-Markt noch besser zu erschließen. Allerdings dürfe das ​nicht zulasten der deutschen Werke gehen. "Wachstum in Amerika muss Arbeit und Wertschöpfung hier sichern und darf sie nicht verdrängen." Das Stammwerk des Volkswagen-Konzerns, zu dem Audi gehört, befindet sich im niedersächsischen Wolfsburg.

IG-Metall-Chefin Christiane Benner, die auch stellvertretende Aufsichtsratsvorsitzende bei Volkswagen ist, forderte verbindliche Perspektiven für die Beschäftigten ​an allen Standorten. Ein Sprecher der IG Metall sagte, es sei mehr als befremdlich, wenn ​sich ein deutscher Auto-Boss zum Wahlkampfhelfer eines US-Präsidenten mache, ⁠der aus deutschen Autobauern amerikanische machen wolle.

"Trump hat nie verhehlt, worum es ihm geht: Er will unsere heimische Wirtschaft schwächen, allen voran die Automobilbranche, Wertschöpfung und Arbeitsplätze sollen über den ⁠Atlantik wandern, seine Zollpolitik ist dafür das Werkzeug", sagte ​der IG-Metallvertreter. "Dass Gernot Döllner ausgerechnet in dieser Lage Trump die Hand reicht, ist schwer zu begreifen. Zumal sein eigenes Unternehmen hierzulande Werke und ganze Regionen infrage stellt."

Das Volkswagen-Management und der Betriebsrat streiten derzeit über den weiteren Kurs bei dem Autokonzern. VW-Chef Blume will bis 2030 weltweit weitere 50.000 Arbeitsplätze streichen. Ab 2030 steht die Produktion in vier deutschen Werken zur Debatte. Es geht auch um die Audi-Fabrik in Neckarsulm.

Redaktionsschluss 17.30 Uhr (MEZ). Dieser Artikel wird nicht weiter aktualisiert!

#3122. Namibia' Black Rhino Is One of Africa’s Most Exciting Conservation Stories

We’d been looking for nearly five hours—scanning the dry grass for upturned pebbles and the horizon for a dark, lumbering shape—when we spotted the rhino bull. Next to me, tracker Mensley Karutjaiva shook his head with a resigned chuckle. We’d been up since daybreak, alongside three other members of the Save the Rhino Trust, looking for critically endangered black rhinos in Damaraland, a land of flat-topped mountains, rocky hills, and stark plains in the Kunene region of northwestern Namibia. While we scrambled about in the heat, the bull had been dozing in the shade of a milk bush. We watched as he stood up, as if posing for us, before lying back down to resume his nap.

I was traveling in a loop around northwest Namibia that took me to three new (or recently renewed) camps. Since photographic safari tourism began here over a century ago, Namibia has largely been known amongst international travelers as a self-drive vacation—a destination with plenty of do-it-yourself road trips through otherworldly desert landscapes. Unknown to many visitors, though, human-wildlife conflict—population activities that negatively affect animals through habitat loss or resource competition— has been an ongoing challenge, and past decades of poaching threw rhinoceros populations into a tailspin. But, in the past few years, Namibia has seen a lot of positive conservation momentum, with these northwest reaches acting as a bellwether. From innovative government measures to an Indigenous-stewarded private reserve, this region is now a story of forward-looking change.

In May 2026, the country’s government launched a new conservation finance deal called Namibia for Life in partnership with the World Wildlife Fund. Worth approximately $63 million, it sets aside funds for Namibia’s 86 community-supported conservancies, which span more than 49 million acres.

Gazing at the black rhino, I felt a thrill as my eyes followed the magnificent swoop of the animal’s two intact horns, the larger of the two over three feet long. In an attempt to deter poachers, wildlife management will often cut down a rhinos’ horns—a humane, if humbling, procedure—so that the sight of a rhino, especially one of this critically endangered sub-species, often entails seeing just nubs.

Wild black rhino populations in Namibia came close to extinction in the mid 90s, but thanks to conservation efforts, have recovered.

Rhino horn can fetch up to $5,400 a pound in the illegal wildlife trade—so just seeing this intact horn was a triumph in itself. Decimated by poaching throughout the 1970s and 80s due to demand in Asia and the Arabian Peninsula, wild black rhino populations had dropped by 96 percent in the mid 90s. They came so close to extinction that only 2,410 were left on the entire planet.

That sighting was a harbinger of better days to come. Thanks to conservation efforts, today some 6,000 black rhinos now roam free, with an estimated half of those living in Namibia. In July 2026, the World Wildlife Fund announced that Namibia had experienced a 53 percent drop in rhino poaching since 2024, reaching its lowest numbers of rhino poaching instances in over a decade.

“Over the past 39 years of visiting Namibia regularly, I’ve been heartened to see a growing understanding of the importance of conservation and community-based tourism–and more recently, how to sustain it,” said travel advisor Nicola Shepherd, who arranged my itinerary. “Namibian organizations such as the nonprofit Integrated Rural Development and Nature Conservation are investing in practical ways to reduce conflict and protect both people and wildlife.”

The founder of the conservation-focused Explorations Company, Shepherd and her team have been arranging customized trips to this country for decades. In the past few years, she’s noted a shift in the advent of luxury accommodations, including Zannier Hotels, Habitas, and others, that have broadened the country's appeal to travelers seeking high-end experiences. It’s all welcome news for the country, which is experiencing an uptick in traveler interest—just two months after my trip, local publications reported that the country’s hotel room occupancy was having one of its strongest years.

More than twice the size of California, Namibia’s expanse includes coastline, semi-arid desert, dry and wet woodlands, and golden savanna. The northwest corner is a rich mix of these terrains–including the otherworldly, white-tipped grasslands I walked through as we returned to Wilderness Desert Rhino Camp after our morning rhino sighting.

“In this area of the Palmwag Concession, the rotation of tourism helps protect rhinos,” Karutjaiva told me. I heard this sentiment echoed wherever I went, although specific numbers are carefully guarded. “It’s a state secret. The only thing I can say is that the population is growing,” he said. The guardedness is understandable, as the threat of poaching is still everywhere. And growth isn’t restricted to the rhino populations.

The lounge area at the intimate Wilderness Desert Rhino Camp, which was formerly a field station for wildlife research.

At Desert Rhino Camp, a full rebuild in 2024 resulted in six stand-alone tented suites, where materials like timber, leather, and stone evoke a modern, desert-chic style. With the Etendeka Mountains rising in the distance, red basalt boulders form the walls of the open-air dining lounge where I enjoyed a delicious slow-cooked lamb potjie stew for dinner.

“We really realized how important tourism was during the pandemic, when most businesses closed and a lot of people lost their jobs,” lodge manager Fanus Katondoka told me that evening, as we sipped on rooibos iced teas. “Now we know it. Operators are building a lot of facilities, lodges, hotels–it's booming.” That growth means more resources for community-supported conservation, so Karutjaiva and his team can continue their work protecting rhinos and other species.

Rhinos have adorned conservation headlines recently, but they’re far from the only draw in northwest Namibia. A new camp from outfitter Natural Selection recently opened about an hour’s flight north, in the desert Kaokoveld region, highlighting another species of desert-adapted wildlife. A few days later, I was headed to see it.

Sitting in the cockpit seat of a tiny four-seater Cessna, I waved goodbye to the rhino trackers and flew over the Namib Desert, one of the oldest arid regions in the world. The landscape flowed in ridges and mountains of faded yellow and whisper pink, where welwitschia–large, low-lying plants that live to be hundreds of years old–sprawled like relics from a Dune film set.

The Namib Desert stretches along the Atlantic coast of Namibia and features some of the highest sand dunes in the world.

It was a fitting introduction to my next stop: the Hoanib Valley, home to a new camp with a mission that’s a bit bigger than rhinoceros. I quickly learned what that something was when guide Howard Groenewaldt picked me up from the airstrip, nosed our vehicle toward camp, and then abruptly cut the engine.

We had stopped at the Hoanib River, a seasonal waterway that runs west from the desert until it reaches the Skeleton Coast on the Atlantic Ocean. Now in the dry season, it turned into a sandy avenue lined with camelthorn and giant ana trees that age more than 100 years old. It was a nature-born runway—and something was stirring around a bend.

A giant, desert-adapted elephant slowly walked toward us. His long tusks were pale white; the bottoms of his feet splayed out wide under his skinny legs, the better to stabilize on sand. There was no sound except the breeze that was stirring the wizened, banana-shaped seed pods in the branches above my head, and the soft fwump of the elephant’s footsteps. I watched, transfixed, as he paused and delicately trailed the tip of his trunk on the ground

The author looks on at a desert-adapted elephant.

This is exactly the kind of encounter the new Natural Selection Hoanib Elephant Camp was built around. Located off the northern bank of the river, its 10 solar-powered suites bring a new level of comfort to the valley. Between game drives, guests here lounge on the veranda overlooking the desert hoping to spy more elephants, or relax in their suites with a massage or nap. Each of the natural-hued suites include a private plunge pool, a deep king-size bed, and a writing desk. Dinner is a multi-course affair in the shared lounge; afterward, a bonfire crackles under the stars. During my stay some visitors curled up on the couches against the evening desert chill, sipping hot drinks spiked with Amarula, a South African liqueur, surrounded by flickering candles.

While the resort is stylish and deeply comfortable, Natural Selection’s approach to community partnership also reflects the shift in this region toward local collaboration. The evening I arrived at camp, I met Petty Daniels, who hails from the nearby Torra Conservancy and brings an acutely local perspective. Years ago, she began working in hospitality as a waitress—today, she’s the manager of both this new camp and its giraffe-focused sister property, the Hoanib Valley Camp. Roughly 97 percent of staff are from surrounding community conservancies, creating a working model of local investment in supporting companies that protect their homeland.

Sundowner drinks are accompanied by a knowledgable guide on a Hoanib Elephant Camp safari.

Within Daniels’ generation, change has been flourishing in the hearts and minds of locals. “Before tourism, people drove wherever they wanted, shooting animals to provide food for their families,” she said. “Not anymore. People are getting into conservation tourism and understanding why they shouldn’t do that.”

Even so, human-wildlife conflict is still a problem. Elephants ramble into gardens and cheetahs kill the occasional goat, but the overall opinion of locals is shifting, especially as the Namibian government signals support for conservation that’s more in step with local interests.

Namibia’s Hoanib Valley was made famous by its desert-adapted elephants.

“Namibia’s communities already help look after lions, elephants, rhinos, water, plants and open land,” Rob Moffett explained a few days later, soon after I arrived at my third and final destination in northwest Namibia. “Instead of saying, ‘Good luck finding money every year,’ the Namibia for Life fund says, ‘Here is a proper money box, a rule book, and a team to help you keep protecting nature.’”

Moffett is the general manager of sales and marketing at Ongava Game Reserve, a private stretch of savanna and mopane woodlands that stretches more than 74,000 acres next to Etosha National Park, one of the country’s biggest visitor attractions. It’s also the site of another positive–and long overdue–story of change.

Wilderness operates single-engine flights from camp to camp that come in handy.

To reach it, I rented a gas-electric hybrid car from ATI Holidays and drove east from the Hoanib Valley. This type of travel is still popular in Namibia, with strong infrastructure supporting visitors. After a detailed briefing with an ATI staff member, I was kitted out with a folder packed with maps, a Namibian cell phone pre-programmed with key contacts, and a first-aid kit. I drove for several hours, listening to music and watching the desert scenery flash by. But driving solo can be tiring, and I was glad for the air travel between previous camps, seamlessly arranged by the Explorations Company. For US travelers unaccustomed to driving on the left side of the road, and often juggling a few shorter vacations, brief single-engine flights from camp to camp are increasingly preferred by high-end resorts opening in the northwest.

The new Horizon by Ongava, where I had arrived, is one of those. Consisting of just three hilltop villas, the property debuted from renovations and a name reset in April 2026, and prioritizes seclusion and comfort. My accommodations unfolded in a graceful series of rooms with couches, a four-poster bed, and a dual-vanity bathroom. Outside, overlooking a plain of the greenest scenery I’d seen so far, a wide deck hugged the hillside, and included two dining spaces, an outdoor shower, and a private plunge pool. My favorite touch was the large bench in the walk-in dressing room, carved in the shape of a serval—a wild cat native to sub-Saharan Africa.

The dining, led by chef Festus Ilyambo, is a feat in its own right. That evening, in the open-air lounge, I asked Ilyambo for the recipes behind his potato pavé—thinly sliced, buttery potatoes infused with organic thyme and fresh cream, then sautéed with garlic—and filet mignon, served with a jus and cognac sauce.

Raised walkways lead to villas at the new Horizon by Ongava.

Alfresco dinners at Horizon by Ongava are a highlight of the day.

The next morning, longtime guide Pascalis Kazimbu led me on a private walking safari in Ongava Game Reserve, pointing out everything from lilac-breasted rollers to white rhino mothers with calves tottering close behind and wildebeest that bleated—“gnuu, gnuu”. Later that afternoon we set out on a game drive, pausing at a watering hole for sundowner drinks as dusk began to settle. I was sipping a local Tafel lager in the safari vehicle when a deep, reverberating roar sounded behind me, vibrating in my bones. Moments later, a male lion strolled out of the thin trees behind me, settling by the pond for a drink.

With game viewing like this, verdant Ongava and its neighboring Etosha National Park are a welcome coda to the scenes of desert and dunes I’d left behind. The circular route of my trip is a popular one: travelers arrive into the capital of Windhoek, travel in a rough circle that begins with awe-inspiring desert and ends near Etosha, and then head back to Windhoek to begin their journey home.

A new community-led concession and lodge in Ongava will protect thousands of acres and help conserve species, including the Southern Yellow-billed Hornbill.

But a coming Ongava project will add a new element for curious travelers. In 2024, the Indigenous San (or Bushmen) Hai//om community and Ongava agreed to a legally binding agreement to partner to manage a new concession, including a new lodge, on the southeast border of Etosha National Park. Slated to open in 2028, the new reserve will secure thousands of acres of land, strengthening wildlife corridors and helping to protect an important waterway called the !Gobaob Spring, where black rhinos and many other species gather to drink in the dry season.

“That place is a miracle,” Ongava manager Moffett said. “In the Hai//om’s oral history, it’s the one spring that has never failed in their entire existence.”

The implications of this new initiative, called the Ongava-Hai//om Joint Venture, extend far beyond wildlife. The concession carries profound historical significance because it includes part of the ancestral homeland of the Hai//om people, who were forcibly removed from Etosha by the government in the 1950s. “Today, most Hai//om people are living scattered on the outside of the park, but this is their home,” Moffett said. “For them to reclaim management and ownership of this concession has the potential to become one of the most special community conservation stories of our time.”

When the 25-year joint venture ends in 2049, the entire business will transfer from its current shared management to the Hai//om community. By protecting endangered wildlife while empowering the Hai//om people to build long-term prosperity, the new concession promises benefits for all involved, including visitors.

The stakes are high for the black rhino, but now, there is also hope.

Learning about this project felt like an encapsulation of everything I’d seen and heard on this journey in northwest Namibia, where community-focused conservation held hands with high-end hospitality. I thought back to the rhino I’d seen in my first days here, and the slow but steady work locals like Karutjaiva are doing to protect the species from the knife’s edge of extinction.

In Namibia, the stakes are high, but so is the promise of a more prosperous, wild future.
