#3124. Last Month Was the Hottest July on Record for the World's Oceans

Oceans globally recorded their hottest July ever last month, according to a new analysis. The severe ocean heat was evident around Europe, which is coping with a scorching summer.

Outside the polar regions, the ocean surface averaged 69.73 degrees F (20.96 degrees C), breaking the previous July record, set in 2023, according to the Copernicus Climate Change Service. Ocean heat was particularly severe in the Pacific, thanks to an intensifying El Niño.

Land surface temperatures were only slightly less severe. Across both land and sea, last month was the second hottest July on record globally.

Daily sea surface temperatures between the 60th parallel south and 60th parallel north. Copernicus Climate Change Service / Adapted by Yale Environment 360

In Europe, heat records continue to fall. “July 2026 was the third consecutive month of exceptional heat in Western Europe, bringing the combined temperature for June and July to a new record for the region,” said Samantha Burgess, deputy director of Copernicus.

Recurrent hot spells and meager rainfall have desiccated soils across Western Europe, making heat more severe. “As soils dry out, they lose their ability to provide natural cooling, allowing heat to build more readily,” Burgess said. “This is a clear example of how climate change is intensifying heat extremes, with heat and drought increasingly reinforcing one another.”

Drought has also drained rivers across Europe. Low water levels on the Danube River, on the border between Serbia and Romania, have revealed dozens of World War II shipwrecks. The ships belonged to a Nazi fleet that was sunk near the end of the war to slow the advance of Soviet forces. Elsewhere on the Danube, in Slovakia, authorities recovered an unexploded WWII bomb.

Overshoot: The World Is Hitting Point of No Return on Climate

#3125. Cookie theft: how fraudsters get the digital key to your accounts

You’ve had a busy morning of life admin, logging into various accounts to check balances and make payments. At the end of a productive few hours you close your laptop and move on to your next job.

“They don’t need your password, they don’t trigger a login alert, and they can often bypass your multifactor authentication (MFA).”

It looks like a regular session on the web. At some point you will have logged in and given your password, and maybe used MFA to prove it’s you.

If it’s a social media or email account you may discover it’s been used by scammers to trick other people. If it’s your bank they could access your account, or they could use details they find to perpetrate an impersonation scam.

And if you were shopping online, the fraudsters could change and redirect your order and charge it to your saved payment details.

You may be alerted by a security alert from a site, for example, a message asking if you have tried to change any details.

Be really careful about the extensions and software you download, because that’s where infostealer malware will usually be hidden.

Look out for signs that your laptop or phone has been infected with malware. Briedis says red flags include your device suddenly struggling with simple tasks, a battery that drains faster than it should, frequent crashes, or a browser that starts behaving oddly with redirects, pop-ups, or a changed homepage.

He suggests moving passwords out of your browser and into a dedicated password manager.

If you suspect you have been compromised, run an antivirus scan. Also log out of your accounts on every device and change your passwords.

Use MFA on accounts, as it does still offer more protection than a simple password.

Alert your bank if you notice any suspicious payments.

#3126. What is the £100k income ‘cliff-edge’?

The tax-free personal allowance starts to be taken away when you hit six figures, and in England you lose access to childcare subsidies

Believe it or not, there are downsides to earning more than £100,000 a year – particularly if you have small children.

“It’s the ambition of many people to reach an income of £100,000, but it does come with an unexpected sting in the tail,” says Sean McCann, a chartered financial planner at NFU Mutual.

Those who make it into six figures face a 60% tax bill on some income. That is because the tax-free personal allowance starts to be taken away once you earn more than £100,000.

For every £2 of income over that, you lose £1 of the £12,570 annual tax-free personal allowance.

“This means that any earnings between £100,000 and £125,140 are effectively taxed at 60%,” says McCann. “When you add on the 2% national insurance payable, that means you only keep £38 of every £100 earned.”

An estimated 800,000 people are in this position, according to a freedom of information request by NFU Mutual.

In England, high earners also lose access to government childcare subsidies once a parent has an adjusted net income of more than £100,000 for a tax year.

As a result, a family with two young children loses roughly £30,000 of financial support if either parent earns even £1 more than the threshold, according to the investment platform AJ Bell.

There are three components to this: tax-free childcare; free hours’ childcare; and child benefit.

AJ Bell’s maths is for a family with a nine-month-old baby and a two-year-old, and puts a value of £4,000, £23,872, and £2,337 on the respective elements.

Sarah Coles, the platform’s head of personal finance, says parents “lose a big chunk” of support as soon as they hit the earnings limit: “It isn’t tapered away gradually, hence the ‘cliff-edge’ moniker.”

All entitlement to tax-free childcare (worth up to £2,000 a child a year) goes, Coles explains.

You also forgo the 30 hours funded term-time childcare for children aged nine months old to three years old, and half the 30 hours for children aged between three and four.

And at this level, all the family’s child benefit entitlement (currently £27.05 a week for your eldest or only child, and £17.90 for each additional child) is clawed back by the high-income child benefit charge (a tax “penalty” that kicks in when someone’s income exceeds £60,000).

So how many missed out on funded childcare because a parent exceeded the income threshold?

Official government data suggests that between 50,500 and 99,000 children in 2025-26. That is according to a freedom of information figures obtained by the wealth management firm Quilter. That is up sharply on just two years earlier, when it was estimated at between 15,600 and 30,800.

#3128. Walking Reindeer Challenge

These walking reindeer made us laugh so much. They work brilliantly, but can be a little fiddly to get going. This activity is traditionally called the walking horse, but as it’s nearly Christmas, we’ve made reindeer!

The reindeer *walk* down a ramp or slope, because of a combination of forces including gravity and friction.

Print the template below to make this activity even easier! If you don’t have a printer, just draw the shape on paper.

Cut the small triangles from the bottom of each leg, and then cut up the legs, stopping at the middle section.

Bend the four legs down and fold the tail upwards.

Fold up the head and again at the top to make a head.

Set up a ramp from a stack of books and thick cardboard, and place the reindeer at the top.

If it doesn’t move, gently push the legs until it starts walking.

When the horse is at the top of the ramp, it has potential energy due to its height. As it moves, this is converted into kinetic energy.

The small triangles you cut at the bottom of the reindeer’s legs make it slightly unstable, so it rocks from side to side when gently pushed. As the reindeer rocks onto one leg, the other legs are lifted up, as it is on an incline, gravity pulls the lifted legs forward.

The reindeer rocks from side to side, moving forward with each cycle, which creates a walking type motion.

If you like this activity, don’t forget to check out my other Christmas science activity ideas.

#3130. Großbrand am Kehlsteinhaus: Feuer wieder aufgeflammt

Der Brand an Hitlers Kehlsteinhaus im früheren Nazi-Sperrgebiet Obersalzberg war gelöscht. Nun musste die Feuerwehr in der Nacht erneut ausrücken.

Nach dem Großbrand am historischen Kehlsteinhaus auf dem Obersalzberg in Berchtesgaden in Bayern ist das Feuer in der Nacht zum Sonntag erneut aufgeflammt. Es habe "nochmal zimmerhoch gebrannt", sagte ein Polizeibeamter. Die Feuerwehr rückte aus und löschte die Flammen. Dass ein Feuer wegen Glutnestern nach dem eigentlichen Brand wieder aufflackert, sei nicht unüblich, sagte er.

Am mehr als 1800 Meter hoch gelegenen Kehlsteinhaus war in der Nacht zum Samstag ein Großbrand ausgebrochen. Nach Angaben des Polizeipräsidiums Oberbayern Süd in Rosenheim löste ein Rauchmelder des Gebäudes am Freitagabend gegen 21.15 Uhr Brandalarm aus. Die alarmierten Feuerwehren sahen bei der Anfahrt hoch aufschlagende Flammen, vor Ort wurde dann "der Vollbrand des Gebäudes feststellt". Die Flammen hätten es "weitestgehend zerstört".

Etwa 200 Rettungs- und Einsatzkräfte von Feuerwehr, Rotem Kreuz, Polizei und Bergwacht waren ausgerückt. Der Schaden geht nach ersten Schätzungen des Zweckverbands Bergerlebnis Berchtesgaden in die Millionen.

Einsatzkräfte nahmen einen 33-jährigen Verdächtigen fest, der aufgrund mutmaßlicher psychischer Erkrankung vorläufig in ein psychiatrisches Krankenhaus eingewiesen wurde. Es würden mögliche politische Motive geprüft, hieß es am Samstag von der Staatsanwaltschaft. Der Mann war laut Ermittlern am Freitagabend unmittelbar nach Brandausbruch von eingesetzten Kräften auf einem Motorrad gestoppt und festgenommen worden.

Das 1937/38 erbaute Kehlsteinhaus bei Berchtesgaden war Teil des sogenannten Führersperrgebietes Obersalzberg und sollte NS-Diktator Adolf Hitler als Repräsentationsgebäude dienen. Seit 1952 wird es als Gaststätte genutzt, eine Ausstellung erinnert an die Geschichte des Hauses. Bayerns Innenminister Joachim Herrmann kündigte bereits den Wiederaufbau an. Das Haus sei wichtig für die "Erinnerungsarbeit".

#3132. Mountain Lions Are Keeping Deer Off the Road, Study Finds

A puma crossing a road on the Olympic Peninsula in Washington state. Nicolás Lagos / Panthera

Mountain lions in Washington state may be cutting down on traffic accidents by scaring deer away from roads, new research finds.

Deer often avoid open spaces, including roads, when predators are nearby. But while it has long been suggested that predators might tamp down on traffic collisions with prey, that has never been demonstrated in areas where big predators are well established, researchers say.

The new study tracked wildlife across the Olympic Peninsula in Washington state, comparing collision data with photos from camera traps that revealed the whereabouts of mountain lions. The big cats, sometimes referred to as cougars or pumas, prey primarily on deer.

Researchers found that where mountain lions were active, deer avoided roads. Overall, deer in those areas were 15 percent less likely to be found near roads, which led to a 67 percent drop in the odds of a traffic accident involving deer. The findings were published in the journal Current Biology.

“When we talk about pumas, we often focus on the risks they pose,” said coauthor Mark Elbroch of Panthera, a conservation group working to preserve wild cats. “But we rarely stop to consider the many ways they benefit us as well.”

Deadly Encounter: Mountain Lion Attacks Spark Controversy

As a graphic designer by trade (hereâs the hard proof), Iâve been using many of these apps and software in one form or another my entire professional career. Whether blogging-specific or simply design and photography related, they work together.

Back in the day, these were typically only available on desktops. But nowadays most of the programs have extremely capable apps that allow me to work seamlessly while mobile, which is pretty handy.

Iâm literally writing this sentence while on my tablet…on a train from Barcelona to Valencia, Spain.

Of course, the first thing you need if youâre starting a blog or site is to have a home for it. Thatâs where a host (or server) comes in. If youâre starting a new blog, you can definitely go with a big gun like Bluehost, Hostgator, or similar. Iâve used both in the past.

Many host providers like Bluehost or Hostgator have “free hosting” or extremely cheap “shared” hosting options that are great tools for beginner bloggers.

Just keep in mind that shared hosting means your site is on a server with potentially hundreds of other sites. This can cause speed issues as well as vulnerability issues.

Eventually I needed a more personal touch and the speed and security that comes with dedicated server hosting. Now I use a smaller, independent host, but they’re attentive and availableâbut not taking new clients at the moment.

Not having a lot of money means that going with free or cheap hosting with Bluehost, Hostgator, or similar is necessary when youâre just starting a blog or website.

As with half the world, I use WordPress for the bones of this website. If you looked at my site pre-2022, it looked different. Before the current design, I used an Elegant Themes page builder. They have many good options and a pretty fantastic âpay for lifeâ plan for all of their WordPress themes.

But be careful with page builders, as they can leave a lot in the background which makes changing themes really difficult. They’re a little more geared toward bonafide websites than blogs.

Now I use the Kadence theme and keep it simple, allowing the words and the photography to shine…hopefully.

For keyword research and SEO optimization researchâwhich are really, really, really important, I use Keysearch almost primarily. It allows me to do all of my own research, make lists of keyword topics, and also spy on some other sites and what theyâre doing. I use this before writing any content because keywords are literally what people may type into a search engine.

Knowing what people are searching for helps you write, so this is easily one of the most important and best blogging tools for beginners.

There are plenty of others, but they tend to cost more than Keysearch does.

I use the Yoast SEO plugin in the backend of WordPress to optimize posts when theyâre actually going into the blog. Yoast has free tools, but I pay a little more for robust WordPress tools that can help me when creating and pre-publishing posts inside WordPress.

Essentially, it’s like having a checklist built into your WordPress builder so that you don’t miss any important things before hitting publish.

Yoast SEO is a great free blogging tool, but well worth the yearly cost if you’re committed.

Google Analytics is pretty much the industry standard for understanding how traffic is getting to, spending time on, or leaving your blog or website. And since Google pretty much runs the world of SEO, why not use this free blogging tool?

You have the option to dive extremely deep into your traffic, down to demographics, time on page, bounce rates, and anything you would like to know. One of the best things is that you can leave notes on your timelines in tools like Google Analytics to mark important events.

For instance, I marked a note when I launched the redesign of this site. Then I noticed that over the next few months my “time on page” per reader almost doubled!

That means the hundreds of hours and financial investment were well worth it! Validation for the win!

Skillshare has been pivotal to me in learning new software and improving my skills in video, social media, and all the other things you need if youâre a creative. I’ve been using Skillshare for years and continue to go back to the well when I need schooled on both old and new software. Seriously…

There are some wicked smart and extremely polished educators on this platform. Highly recommend.

For photography, which Iâm passionate about but still have a lot to learn about, I almost always spend a couple of minutes editing or processing before you see anything. Whether it’s published in blog posts on this site, on Instagram, or any other social media platform.

One day I hope thatâs one of the things that makes this site stand out from the restâattention to quality photography.

The software I use for photo and video is from the Adobe Creative Suite, specifically Lightroomâboth on laptop and mobile. Lightroom allows me to take shots on mobile, import, edit and use, then syncs with my account so I can continue on the laptop. I also use if for RAW photos from my DSLR because those take some horsepower.

Organization is key when you have a lot of photos and destinations, and Lightroom is also built for organization. Beyond that, I also use Premiere Pro for serious video edits for YouTube.

Itâs one of the industry standards for video editing. On mobile, Iâll use Adobe Rush for quick edits for Instagram or YouTube shorts.

When posting to YouTube, I highly recommend TubeBuddy, since it makes âchecking all the boxesâ of best practices easy. TubeBuddy helps with SEO, keywords, card insertion, end screens, etc., and can also make batch changes to descriptions, which means you save time. (You can see The Nomad Experiment YouTube channel by clicking the icon below.)

I saw a significant improvement in people seeing my videos in search resultsâthus more viewersâonce I started using their interface. I suggest you start with a small, inexpensive plan then go up once you know whether YouTube is your jam.

TubeBuddy kind of ended up being a blogging tool too, since I learned a lot about SEO and keywords by using the Tubebuddy YouTube plugin.

Similar to photography, I use Adobe Creative Suite for page layout, and I have been for a long time. As much as I didnât like Adobeâs subscription model in the beginning, Iâve been won over by it because I literally have all the software and apps I need for so many needs, both on laptop and mobile.

I use Adobe InDesign 90% of the time for page layout and print design. It allows me to add hyperlinks throughout designs and create fillable fields that all export out to PDFs that end up being freebies on this site.

Oh, and I designed my entire 300+ page âBeginner Travelerâs Guide To Going Nomadâ 100% with Adobe InDesign.

I also use Canva occasionally, as it’s free and easy to use. But I use Canva mostly for image thumbnails or Instagram slideshows. Adobe also has “Adobe Express” which is similar to Canva, and it’s included in a Creative Suite subscription, or as a standalone.

I use Quip a lot for drafting contentâI’m literally drafting this article in Quip. Probably because itâs just an old go-to for me and I donât like how much control Google has over my data, so I don’t use Google docs much. I’ll then copy and paste into other tools like Grammarly. I use Grammarly for…well…grammar, punctuation, and tone issues.

I really like how Grammarly allows me to choose the tone of the article I’m writing and keeps me on track with that.

For much more broad, long-form content like writing The Beginner Travelerâs Guide to Going Nomad, I use Scrivener. They have large manuscript organization and tools on lock. Itâs kind of like Word on steroids. Not too many bells and whistles or sexy UI design, but it really helps with clearing away and allowing you to organize and write long-form content.

Regardless of whether you travel all of the time or are a nomad, you should have a VPN running at all times when using public wifi networks. Itâs literally a blogging tool beginners often overlook. But using a VPN is super helpful, since a âlack of stressâ is what it gives you. If you’re logging into your precious blog and a hacker gets your password, good luck!

And I know youâre using public wifi networks all the timeâbecause I do! (I’m literally writing this sentence from Metalo Cafe in Cape Verde, Africa on a public network.) Itâs so ridiculously easy for a hacker to get your passwords and content, yet itâs also ridiculously inexpensive to simply use a VPN nonstop.

I’m currently using Surfshark VPN and I’ve been very happy with it, but Iâve used ExpressVPN in the past and also recommend. Pretty much all VPNs will also give you the ability to use your VPN plan on at least 3 devices simultaneously, on the same plan.

Surfshark VPN has a feature where I can turn on âKill Switchâ which wonât even allow me to use the web unless the VPN is connected, which is a brilliant safeguard!

Obviously, I just implored you to get…and use…a VPN. But literally on the same level of concern should be using a password manager. If youâre still using some whack combination of your birthday or your last dogâs nameâin only 2-3 different variationsâthen if a hacker gets one of your passwords itâs not long before they have access to everything.

The best password managers will help you make every password unique and robust, then autofill fields for you so you never have to remember those super-tricky passwords.

This is a great blogging tool for beginner bloggers because starting a blog means a lot of new passwords and logins! Only $20-30 a year to save you the possibility of some major headachesâand potential financial loss or credit report havocâseems like a small price to pay.

Iâve used 1Password for years, but there are many reputable password managers out there. Find the one that fits you!

One thing I was extremely bad about back when I was new to blogging was growing an email list. This is the second most important thing to do next to owning your blog or website, which allows you to control all of your intellectual property. If Instagram, Facebook, or YouTube goes away, you still have your blog and your content. But…

without an email list of past readers, you’ll have very limited ways of contacting them and monetizing!

I’ve been using Mailerlite for a couple of years now and really like it. Mailerlite is a simple, drag-and-drop email marketing and list management app that has some really good tutorials.

More importantly, they have a free plan that allows you to build a fairly large email list before you need to pay. (Oh…the email optin form below will pop your email directly in my Mailerlite list. Hint, Hint.)

Skip aggressive but useful tools like ConvertKit until you have a substantial list built, since they tend to cost a chunk of change each month.

If youâre going to start a blog, or build any small business, I highly suggest you track your time on major time-sucks. Yes, even if youâre unlikely to make money for a long time. First, itâs good to know where your time is going…or not going.

Second, youâre less likely to lose your mind with the âI feel like Iâve been busy but I feel like I have nothing to showâ head game that happens when you get lost in time-consuming work.

I use Toggl Trackâs free plan for this and itâs more than enough. Itâs definitely one of my favorite blogging tools for beginners, but also just for any small business. I create a project regularly for longer-form posts so I can see how much time from start to publish it takes me. Then when I start hiring out, I at least have a good baseline for the time involved.

For project management I sometimes use Trello, but honestly Iâm sh*t at project management for this travel blog. Often times it just feels like Iâm battling a dumpster fire, haha!

I switched from the cult of MAC when I went nomadic because I didnât like the idea of always being force to pay 100% of an a phone bill if I was going to be on wifi a majority of the time. Apple iPhones aren’t Google fi native, so I jumped ship. Google fi and a Google Pixel was where I went, and no I use a Samsung Galaxy S20 because it allowed for an expansion SD card.

Since Google Fi allows calls on wifi and allows me to use my US number in hundreds of other countries, I rarely have to worry about landing somewhere and getting a SIM card (which means a new number, ugh). No, this isnât technically a blogging tool, but it makes life easier and itâs tech, so it made it on the list!

Google fi still has some kinks to work out. But saving upwards of $300-400 a year because I am smart about my data use makes it well worth the investment.

Especially when our phones can cost as much as a decent computer these days.

Obviously with any blog comes the dream of making âpassiveâ revenue in the background. What facilitates those are affiliate networks. Essentially, companies pay bloggers and others for âmarketingâ their products through referral links. Obviously, I only support products Iâve used or that come highly recommended, so itâs a win-win.

Affiliate networks like Awin, Partnerize, etc., help facilitate those relationships between advertisers and websites. So as you advance your blogging career, youâll want to create relationships with them. Some companies like Safetywing travel insurance run their own affiliate program.

Keep an eye out for great products and pay attention to whether they have an affiliate network they use or if they run their own program. Add these blogging tools to your toolbox only if your blog can actually get readership and clicks.

When you get enough content on your site, reach out to see if youâre a fit for to work with them. I actually donât suggest dipping your toes in this pool yet if youâre just starting a blog. Wait a little while.

The best advice I can give you is to have realistic expectations of how long building something as a second income can take, especially if starting a blog is that second income stream! It takes a lot of time and effort to grow your blog.

Iâve been writing and blogging for over 5 years now and I still only pull enough to pay for the expensesâand barely. Can you do it faster? Absolutely. But…

I suggest setting your expectations for a longer timeline and then putting in the work and maybe being happily surprised when you hit your goals earlier. If youâre starting with a strong social medial following, that will definitely help.

But if you know nothing about keywords, SEO optimization, email funnels, and management, or even how to edit good photos and videos, you likely have much more learning in front of you than you expect.

#3134. The young Americans choosing Y2K cars over shiny new SUVs: ‘I get so much crap from people’

People under 35 are hunting for no-frills rides they can pay for in cash and fix themselves – defying expectations to take on steep loans

Jack Price joined the marines in fall 2021 knowing he would need a car to get around base and that he couldn’t afford a new one. A joyless, month-long search through dealer classifieds and Facebook Marketplace listings ended when his mother spotted a 2010 Volkswagen Jetta posted on her office listserv by a co-worker moving abroad. With just 60,000 miles (96,560km) on the meter, the car (a manual!) was mechanically sound, and the air conditioning, sunroof and Bluetooth radio worked too. At $6,000, it was a steal. Price paid in cash.

Five years on, the Jetta, which he called the Pewter Pebble, is still carting him from A to B in unassuming style. “I’ve completely driven it into the ground,” says the 30-year-old, who lives in Los Angeles. “All over bases, cross-country a couple times, not really lovingly. I get so much crap from people, but not people my age.”

For generations, buying a first new car has been a rite of passage in the US, the clearest sign of a young person responsibly striking out on their own. But younger millennials and gen Z are increasingly finding that path blocked. The average new vehicle costs more than $50,000, an alarming figure that reflects Americans’ insatiable appetite for progressively larger SUVs and pickups. Meanwhile, adults under 35 are disappearing from showroom floors, accounting for less than 10% of new vehicle registrations in 2024 – a new low.

According to the credit reporting agency Experian, the average monthly car payment in the US is $765 for a new car and $542 for a used one, with the loan terms now stretching to six, even seven, years. A record 21% of new-car buyers pay $1,000 or more a month, according to the autos research group Edmunds. And that’s before factoring in maintenance, fluctuating gas prices and the eye-watering insurance premiums often charged to drivers under 35.

For many young Americans already under the strain of student loans, high housing costs and an unforgiving labor market, buying a new car or even a newer used one is out of the question. Enter the Y2K car – the retro ride that has become a trendy romantic choice for some younger Americans. “There’s definitely a golden era of cars from the late 90s and early 2000s that people just really want all of a sudden,” says Charlie Spears, a service adviser at an independently owned repair shop in Atlanta who conducts prepurchase inspections of used cars on demand. “A lot of people just want something that’s not going to fail them.”

Y2K cars don’t encompass everything produced in that era. They are the ones that simply won’t die. Think wedge-shaped Honda Accords and slab-sided Toyota Camrys: dependable, hand-me-down transport that can last 150,000 miles or more with routine maintenance. (Price’s Jetta, which was built at the end of a model production run that began in 2005, just makes the cut.) They’re economical to run and relatively inexpensive to insure, owing to their drastically lower worth. Best of all, the purchase price is low enough to pay in cash. Even when buyers need a loan – at steep interest rates as modern cars have in effect become mortgage payments on wheels – they can pay it off in a fraction of the time it takes to finance a newer car.

The supply of these bargains is dwindling: used vehicles priced under $15,000, the sweet spot for Y2K cars, have shrunk from 21% of inventory a year ago to 15%, according to industry analysis from Cox Automotive – numbers that speak to the demand for affordable cars. “Part of the draw is the nostalgia factor – people wanting cars they grew up with,” says Consumer Reports’ autos expert Keith Barry. “An old car with the right music on is kind of the closest thing to a time machine.”

On social media, the Y2K car has emerged as an ironic status symbol – a marker of maturity, grit and lack of pretense. Defects – busted aircon, glitchy windows, bits and bobs that have failed, fallen off or been held on with gaffer tape – are simply treated as part of a Y2K car’s personality. “I don’t know what kind of disease that is in the brain,” says one TikTok creator, who has to pry open the fuel door of her 2003 Mitsubishi Outlander with a screwdriver. “But I don’t get frustrated. I’m like, ‘Oh, this is fun.’”

In a Y2K car that takes a lickin’ and keeps on tickin’, creature comforts are beside the point. For many, part of the appeal is the absence of dashboard infotainment displays and other screens that have replaced basic switches and knobs in newer cars, often in the name of cost-cutting. Automakers’ zeal to monetize in-car technology, sometimes charging extra to activate physically installed features such as heated seats and remote start, only reinforces young buyers’ doubts about the value of a new car.

At the same time, do-it-yourself repairs have become harder as cars grow more digitally complex and auto manufacturers cut off access to owners and even independent mechanics. Oftentimes, they require investing in specialty digital readers or computer software to diagnose problems, and bespoke tools for wrenching. Most car owners would sooner punt, take the car to the dealership and pay whatever the maintenance and repair costs – which, of course, is exactly the vertical integration that shareholder-bound manufacturers are after.

Hence the romance with Y2K cars: you can fix ’em yourself, their old-school controls fall easily to hand, they’re tried and tested, seemingly built to last. They just have an aura of invincibility – never mind that their age makes them fundamentally more dangerous and susceptible to frequent repairs. “I’ve used my Jetta as if it were a truck,” Price says. “Transported trees in it, put my surfboard through the sunroof. I’ve done every single thing possible to thrash it beyond its abilities.”

Price’s passengers, however, don’t have much appreciation for Pewter Pebble’s quirks. Often while carpooling to US military installations, senior officers compelled to hold up the sagging headliner would ask him when he was trading in his beater for a new truck or SUV. It was a similar story when Price left the military last year and started chauffeuring Alex Vindman, the Iraq war veteran turned US Senate hopeful, on the campaign trail during Florida’s special election. “I drove him around a couple times in it, and he was like, ‘What is going on?’” Price says. Generally speaking, he says, “the fact that we expect folks who are getting paid, like, a grand a month to have a new car is nuts.”

Twenty-five miles north-west of downtown Atlanta, a half hour’s drive in optimistic traffic, sits Town Center at Cobb, a typical suburban mall in recession. But on the first Sunday of every month, the mall parking comes alive for Caffeine and Octane, a sprawling car show that’s open to the public, free to attend and also has long lines for coffee and donut trucks. And even though the usual turnout of 2,000-plus cars was winnowed down to a few hundred on the gray, showery morning of October’s show, it was still an impressive array of vintage American muscle, Japanese domestic market (or JDM) unicorns and multimillion-dollar exotics. The Y2K cars stood out, too.

Sitting right at the entrance of the show was Anthony DeNaples’s 2000 Buick Regal GS, a sleeper sedan that has become a cult classic. He bought it four months ago for $18,000 on Facebook Marketplace, a price he considered a bargain for such a rare commuter. “Person didn’t really know what they had,” says the 37-year-old custom car builder, who uses the car to ferry his two young daughters. His oldest is constantly goading him to do burnouts, which she calls “making stripes”. Modern cars, he reasons, have become so laden with safety systems – for keeping the car in lane, or slamming on the brakes when sensors detect an obstacle – that they’re less fun to drive hard. “A lot of newer cars, if you want to drive it how it’s meant to be driven, you’ve gotta shut off all these things, and a lot of times it doesn’t completely shut off.”

A few rows down from the Buick were Mike and Kate Landaverde in his-and-hers Honda Civics from the 90s: a barebones CRX coupe called Dirty Gertrude and a slightly less spartan wagon called Tatiana. The couple bought them cheap, in keeping with the Y2K ethos. Mike, 34, treats having to bump-start the CRX and his sweat-soaked commutes in summer traffic as part of the thrill ride. “I’m smiling the whole time,” says Mike, who manages a local Wendy’s. They keep a 2021 Toyota Corolla hatchback in the garage as a backstop. That car’s got AC.

When young Wendy’s customers pull up to the drive-through in newer barges he assumes carry huge monthly payments, Mike can’t help feeling a bit smug. The couple’s Hondas, however, haven’t necessarily come cheap. Kate, 39, reckons the money she has put into modifications on Tatiana alone could buy a new car. But the occasional outlays – on an engine swap, most recently – can be done at a self-directed pace. “You can do the mods at your own wallet bracket,” says Kate, a software specialist who does her own wrenching. “You can go eBay parts. But all my friends call me bougie because I’m not gonna do anything cheap.”

Meanwhile, making a case for best in show was Alex Jimenez in a 2001 Volkswagen Eurovan tricked out to look like the Scooby-Doo Mystery Machine. “My family is growing,” said Jimenez, a 32-year-old facilities manager, as his 14-year-old daughter Izel sat shotgun scrolling through her phone while a Scooby-Doo cartoon played on the aftermarket infotainment display. Beyond the extra space, he says, “we love it because I can literally fix everything at home. I don’t need special equipment or tools or a computer to diagnose what’s wrong with it. And it puts smiles on people’s faces everywhere we drive. I mean, everybody’s taking pictures.”

Y2K cars have their charms, sure. Barry, of Consumer Reports calls it “survivorship bias” – seeing the most durable makes and models still on the road contributes to assumptions that all from the era must be rock solid. But there’s a reason the automotive industry moved on. “If you go back to cars in the 1990s, our road-test data and reliability surveys were showing problems that were unheard of on modern vehicles: defective paint, parts falling off; the steering wheel would be on crooked,” Barry says. Rust is another scourge of Y2K cars, though it’s less of a concern in the Sun belt. Newer models are far better protected against corrosion. “The technology that allows a car’s body and frame to survive longer is just so much more advanced,” says Barry, adding that a modern car from a manufacturer that prioritizes reliability can reasonably be expected to last 200,000 miles or more with proper maintenance. To his point: the average car on American roads is now 13 years old.

Still, some young Y2K-car owners welcome the opportunity to get their hands dirty. Che Bidal, a 19-year-old Pit operator who also belongs to the furry community, was idling inside his 2004 Mazda6 as the rain poured on Caffeine and Octane. He takes immense pride in having bought the broken sedan for $900 and getting it running again with another $1,500 in parts and help from AI chatbots and YouTube tutorials. Having Frankensteined a Y2K car back to life, the prospect of taking on a new car payment – in this economy – only to be discouraged by the manufacturer from making his own repairs seems ridiculous. “Car companies are banks now,” says Bidal, who was further emboldened by his positive experience under the hood to start a wrenching group for furries. “Coming from fandom into the [auto enthusiast] community is a whole different thing – pretty amazing.”

Nevertheless, there’s a critical difference between a car that’s gratifying to restore and one that can protect you when something goes terribly wrong. Modern cars are subject to increasingly demanding safety standards, and the differences in structural reinforcements and accident-avoidance features between models just three years apart can be dramatic. Consumer Reports has long recommended that drivers prioritize safety as much as their budget allows – advice that its largely Boomer readership can more easily take onboard, naturally.

Barry went through an old-car phase too. “When I was driving around in my 1988 Renault Medallion, I’d see someone come across the center line as they’re looking on their phone and think, ‘I would not stand a chance in an overlap collision,’” he says. “You might think that the older car you’re driving is a tank. Well, the car might be fine. It’s the things inside the car, the new safety features, that help you survive.” Features like blind-spot monitoring, driver attention monitoring and electric stability control, which intervenes to maintain traction in a skid.

The US auto industry has made some effort to address young consumers’ affordability concerns. In 2021, Ford introduced the Maverick, a four-door compact pickup with an available hybrid powertrain and a starting price below $30,000. A startup called Slate, which offers an infinitely customizable electric truck with a starting price of about $25,000, hopes to begin fulfilling orders at the end of this year.

In China, buyers can get new cars with features that would seem extravagant at the same price point in the US – the payoff from decades of Chinese investment in innovation and American offshoring of manufacturing. An affordable, widely available Chinese car with modern features would give young US car buyers something truly tantalizing to consider. But Chinese automakers are in effect shut out of the US market. Even if they weren’t, Barry cautions: “We’re still not getting one for $8,000. Think closer to $20,000. You’re still going to have a car payment. And even if the interior is simple, it’s a different kind of simplicity than someone driving a 99 Camry is looking for.”

Ultimately, young consumers just want cars that deliver on the industry’s fabled promise of independence – and right now, Y2K cars are meeting the brief. These days, Price’s Pewter Pebble struggles with a leaky sunroof, and the headliner is still drooping. “It’s a Volkswagen so it’s coming back to bite me in the butt,” he says. “I’ve probably had to put three grand into it this year.” But with more than 150,000 miles on the clock, it’s taken him further than even he could have imagined.

The absence of a car note helped him become a homeowner in his 20s. It softened the blow of Alex Vindman’s upset loss to Democratic socialist Angie Nixon in Florida’s August Senate primary. Rather than scramble for another job, Price took a European vacation to decompress and plot his next move. As it happens, he spoke to the Guardian from the Spanish Pyrenees. “If I had a car payment and I lost my job,” he says, “I wouldn’t have the flexibility to hike a mountain.”

#3136. You be the chancellor: play our budget game to see if you can balance the books

See if you can do a better job than John Healey at creating a budget that keeps MPs and the bond markets onside

On 28 October, John Healey will announce the autumn budget to parliament. As in all years, the chancellor has to strike a balance between the following:

Raising the money needed to fund the services that voters demand.

Keeping taxes at levels that are acceptable to voters.

Persuading the government’s creditors in the bond markets that it will continue to be able to pay its debts.

In our game you get to try your hand at designing a successful budget. Can you keep backbenchers happy without upsetting the bond markets? And can you do it all while keeping the books balanced? Just like the real chancellor, you begin in the red.

At the spring statement, Healey’s immediate predecessor, Rachel Reeves, had left herself £23.6bn of “headroom” against the fiscal rules, which require day-to-day spending to be matched by receipts by the end of this parliament. However, the buffer is widely expected to have been slashed by a surge in debt interest costs, as the war in the Middle East triggers a global inflation shock that is rattling the bond markets.

Taken together, the Resolution Foundation thinktank predicts the headroom against the government’s fiscal target will be halved, to about £12bn.

Healey is thought to be willing to allow the buffer to take a hit. However, jittery bond investors are looking for him to retain a degree of headroom to guard against further negative shocks, with at least £14bn thought to be needed to avoid provoking a bond market reaction.

The chancellor is also under pressure to find space in the public finances for three things: a cost of living support package; a boost in defence spending; and financing Andy Burnham’s long-term priority to spread “good growth” to every postcode.

Solving the above is all part of your task. You’ll see the headroom grow or shrink, or – most worrying – become negative in response to your policy decision. But keep an eye on the alerts on how the markets – which may not be convinced – and the opinion polls are responding as you go. Good luck.

If you get stuck tap or click for Treasury briefings – chart icon – and wry notes from Burnham – note icon – for guidance.

Sources and notes: the estimates of income from each of the measures and for the current government balance come from a variety of economists and thinktanks and the government’s own assumptions. The core fiscal impacts are from HM Revenue and Customs, and the estimates for other measures are sourced in the descriptions. These estimates are often based on those economists’ own assumptions about future growth, inflation, collection rates and other variables. So this game should be seen as illustrative of the relative sizes of the impacts of measures. Do not buy or sell gilts on the basis of this game, in case you were thinking of it.

#3137. Fabric user interface can be worn on your sleeve — and machine-washed

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Shanshan Yao is in the Department of Mechanical Engineering, Stony Brook University, Stony Brook, New York 11794, USA.

Writing in Nature, Fu et al.1 report a fabric sleeve that displays graphics, senses touch and environmental conditions, and conducts simple computational tasks, all while stretching and breathing like ordinary cloth. By seamlessly assembling electronic devices directly into fibres, the authors have turned everyday clothing into a graphical user interface (GUI) — a visual display, similar to a smartphone screen, that allows people to interact with electronic devices.

#3138. Cheeky Elf Tricks – STEM Challenges

If your elf needs a bit of inspiration, these cheeky elf tricks might be just the thing. All you need to do is set up the trick and leave an instruction card out for children to solve the puzzle!

The challenge cards are free to save and print!

Print, choose your favourite, and start experimenting.

Leave a mixture of boiled and unboiled eggs with the elf…the challenge is to work out which eggs are which!

The difference is how they spin. The unboiled egg wobbles rather than spins!

Place small pieces of tissue paper on the floor with a balloon nearby.

The idea is to charge the balloon with static electricity to pick up the tissue paper pieces.

For this activity, you need to create some small paper signs. If you place a glass of water in front of them, the arrow will reverse!

We’ve made reversing arrows before with an Alice in Wonderland theme.

Use vinegar to dissolve the shell of an egg and then place it in coloured water!

You should be able to bounce the eggs too, but be careful as they can break!

Make an elf jumping bean! You’ll be amazed by how it jumps and bounces around.

Place a glass over a coin and fill it with water. The coin seems to disappear! This clever trick of light will amaze your friends and family.

Pour a layer of cooking oil over coloured water, seal the jar, shake and watch the colours separate!

As an extension task try to find a small object that will flor on water, but not oil

To make a festive lava lamp, you’ll need coloured water, a jar, oil and an effervescent vitamin tablet. Place the tablet in the jar and watch the fizz.

The great thing about these is that if you keep adding effervescent vitamin tablets, they’ll keep bubbling away.

Can you think of any more cheeky elf tricks for us?

#3140. Mehr als 1000 Festnahmen nach neuen Protesten in Frankreich

Die Demonstrationen in Frankreich reißen nicht ab. Am Samstag gingen wieder Hunderttausende auf die Straße. Die Polizei nahm mehr als 1000 Menschen fest. Allerdings gab es weniger Gewalt als am Wochenende zuvor.

Unklar ist, wie groß die Kundgebungen vom gestrigen Samstag tatsächlich waren. Das Innenministerium sprach von landesweit 410.000 Teilnehmern, die Gewerkschaft CGT, die die Kundgebungen maßgeblich mitorganisiert hatte, nannte deutlich höhere Zahlen. Nach ihren Angaben demonstrierten landesweit fast 800.000 Menschen unter anderem gegen hohe Lebenshaltungskosten und Mängel im Bildungssystem. Laut CGT beteiligten sich allein in der Hauptstadt Paris rund 250.000 Menschen an den Kundgebungen.

Innenminister Laurent Nuñez sagte, die Einsatzkräfte seien "mit einem extrem erhöhten Maß an Gewalt" von "Militanten der extremen Linken" konfrontiert gewesen. Zusammenstöße zwischen Polizei und Protestierenden gab es nach den Worten des Ministers unter anderem in der Hauptstadt Paris, in Lyon, Nantes und Toulouse.

Nuñez betonte zugleich, die genehmigten Protestzüge seien zunächst "gut verlaufen". Es seien mehr Polizeikräfte auf den Straßen präsent gewesen. Diese hätten schneller reagiert, im Vorfeld der Demonstrationen verstärkt Kontrollen durchgeführt und Geschäfte besser geschützt. Es habe zwar einige Plünderungen gegeben, es seien aber deutlich weniger gewesen als in der Woche zuvor.

Bei Zusammenstößen in den Städten Clermont-Ferrand und Toulouse seien allerdings zwei Menschen "schwer verletzt" worden, so der Innenminister weiter. In Toulouse wurde nach Angaben der Behörden ein 20-Jähriger schwer an der Hand verletzt. Ein Helfer sagte, der junge Mann habe bei einer Explosion mehrere Finger verloren.

Die Behörden hatten zur Vorbereitung auf die Proteste strenge Auflagen erlassen und viele Kundgebungen verboten. Außerdem war ein Großaufgebot von etwa 70.000 Sicherheitskräften im Einsatz. Am Wochenende zuvor war es in Frankreich landesweit zu massiven Ausschreitungen gekommen.

Tausende Geschäfte wurden geplündert, Rathäuser und Polizeistationen angegriffen und Gebäude in Brand gesetzt. Die Behörden meldeten rund 2700 Festnahmen am vergangenen Wochenende.

Unterdessen kündigte die Gelbwesten-Bewegung bereits neue Demonstrationen für die kommende Woche an. Am 31. Oktober solle es einen "3. Akt" geben, teilten die Organisatoren mit. Sie riefen zu Kundgebungen und Sitzstreiks vor der Nationalversammlung sowie vor sämtlichen Rathäusern Frankreichs auf.  Am kommenden Freitag soll zudem eine Protestveranstaltung stattfinden, bei der mit Küchenutensilien wie Töpfen und Pfannen Lärm gemacht wird - eine sogenannte Casserolade.

Der Protest der Gelbwesten gegen hohe Lebenshaltungskosten und soziale Ungerechtigkeit hatte Frankreich zwischen November 2018 und dem Frühjahr 2019 in Atem gehalten. Ihr Name geht darauf zurück, dass viele der Demonstranten gelbe Warnwesten trugen.

Die Demonstrierenden in Frankreich gehören nicht zu einer einheitlichen Bewegung. Den jungen Leuten, die seit Wochen zunächst für eine bessere Ausstattung von Schulen demonstriert hatten, haben sich weitere Gruppen angeschlossen, etwa Angehörige verschiedener Berufsgruppen, die Gelbwesten und Vertreter der französischen Linken. Gewerkschaften unterstützen sie dabei. Der linkspopulistische Präsidentschaftskandidat Jean-Luc Mélenchon sagte mit Blick auf die Proteste, Staatschef Emmanuel Macron müsse einsehen, "dass sich die Kundgebungen jede Woche wiederholen" würden. In Frankreich sind für den 18. April und 2. Mai 2027 Präsidentschaftswahlen angesetzt.

Redaktionsschluss 17.30 Uhr (MEZ). Dieser Artikel wird nicht weiter aktualisiert!

#3141. Luxury Upgrades That Will Make Your Bedroom Feel More Like a Five-Star Hotel

If you’ve ever felt like you get the best sleep at a hotel, you’re not alone. It’s a common sentiment amongst travelers, and one I definitely know well. Whether it’s my body sinking into vacation mode, those crisp white sheets, or a little bit of both, there’s a reason why sleeping at a luxury hotel feels deeply restorative in a way that’s tough to achieve on a regular basis.

Recently, I’ve found myself feeling excited to return home after a long trip, but less enthused about the bedroom that awaited me. So, I set out on a mission to emulate the oasis-like feeling of a top-notch hotel at home. I even enlisted the help of hospitality and wellness experts to guide me on which bedroom essentials make all the difference.

Below, I’ve gathered the premium bedding, linens, and bedtime accessories used at some of the best hotels around the world. From cloud-like mattresses to plush robes and slippers, these little luxuries will help make your bedroom feel more like a five-star hotel.

Mattresses are the foundation of a quality sleep experience, and the first thing most of us gleefully plop down on after arriving at a lavish hotel. If there’s an item to splurge on, this is it. The Bryte Balance Smart Bed, featured at Carillon Miami Wellness Resort, is a premium option equipped with smart cushions that adjust firmness throughout the night, relieving pressure on strategic points of your body. Tammy Pahel, VP of spa and wellness at the resort, also recommends the Tempur-Pedic LuxeBreeze, with its cooling technology, as another option worth the price tag.

For all of the luxury at a slightly more accessible price point, the Saatva Classic Luxury Firm mattress (there are three firmness options) is my personal favorite, with just the right amount of sturdiness and back support. If you’re looking for the exact mattress from your favorite stay, keep in mind that several hotel groups sell branded sleep products online, like this Four Seasons Signature mattress.

“Other than bringing a beautiful aesthetic to the key element of a bedroom, quality pillows are a must for optimal sleeping and even just when sitting in bed,” says Karen Smith, wellness director at Six Senses Ibiza, where guests will find Hanse’s firm and fluffy pillows. Smith also points to Naturalmat as an excellent choice for at-home luxury thanks to its variety of goose and duck down pillows, as well as wool for a firmer feel.

I can attest that Saatva’s best-selling latex pillow has the same magical qualities as its Classic Mattress: soft and supportive. Plus, the breathable latex means you’ll always be on the cool side of the pillow. While nothing says luxury quite like the soft, cloud-like feeling of a down pillow, anyone with allergies—or a stricter budget—can find just as much comfort for a fraction of the price with Quince’s Premium Down Alternative pillow. The option to choose between soft, medium, and firm also makes this pillow a noteworthy competitor.

Along with hotel pillows, you'll want to invest in some top-quality linens. And that’s where the brand Brooklinen really shines. “Our Classic Percale Sheets are inspired by that perfectly crisp, luxurious feel we’ve come to know and love from hotel beds,” says Nicolas Lukac, Brooklinen's director of emerging channels, describing these 100% cotton, 270-thread-count sheets. Complete your set with Brooklinen’s Washed Classic Percale comforter for an extra dose of coziness, or opt for the smoother finish of the best-selling Luxury Sateen sheets.

It’s hard to beat the quality to price ratio of Brooklinen, but if your top priority is finding sheets that won’t leave you waking up in a sweat, Pahel recommends Frette products for its “superior comfort, durability, and luxury.” Pahel recommends Nollapelli for its cooling powers. Many hot sleepers also swear by Sijo's AiryWeight cooling eucalyptus bedding. The sheets are moisture-wicking and silky smooth.

Getting the basics right is definitely a great start, but according to Vivianne Garcia-Tunon, vice president of wellbeing for Auberge Resorts Collection, “a five-star experience can be achieved by prioritizing style, comfort, and innovation.” And that means picking the right bedroom accessories.

Whether you need to block out the noise of a snoring partner, a busy street, or even your busy mind, a simple, easy-to-use sound machine like the LectroFan EVO can help lull you to sleep. Options range from white, brown, and pink noise to electronic fan sounds, but if you want something with more features, consider the Hatch Restore, which serves as a sunrise-imitating alarm, as well as a sound machine with the option for guided meditations and bedtime stories.

Garcia-Turon also recommends avoiding technology with a charging station like Aro that keeps your phone out of reach, while using dimmable lights to create an inviting and soothing ambiance that primes your brain for sleep well before bedtime. A proper eye covering like the Alaska Bear Silk Sleep Mask will also ease you into dreamland, while a good set of blackout curtains will help you stay there.

Garcia-Turon also recommends avoiding technology with a charging station that keeps your phone out of reach, while using dimmable lights to create an inviting and soothing ambiance that primes your brain for sleep well before bedtime. A proper eye mask like this one made with organic silk will also ease you into dreamland, while a good set of blackout curtains will help you stay there.

Take things up a notch by using diffusers or candles to fill your space with enchanting aromas. Chrissy Fichtl, founder of home fragrance brand Apotheke, recommends layering a variety of scents throughout the home: clean and crisp for the bedroom; bright and savory for the kitchen; and fun, seasonal fragrances for the bathroom. “White Vetiver is the ultimate luxury scent,” Fichtl says, but Canvas and Charcoal are other popular options with an upscale feel.

You can also lean on scents that transport you to specific moments from your travels like the Mediterranean-inspired products from Acqua di Parma; the Glasshouse Fragrances that evoke memories of Rio de Janeiro, Kyoto, and beyond; or the oddly specific perfumes, candles, and more by DS & Durga like Big Sur After Rain or Wild Brooklyn Lavender.

Every luxury hotel wows its guests with cozy robes and slippers, so now that your bedroom is all set for sleep success, it’s time to upgrade your gear. Bathrobes come in many materials, but for something classic, try The Company Store’s cotton long robe, made with plush terry-cloth that’s soft to the touch and also absorbent, making it perfect to throw on right after a shower. For something a bit cooler that exudes the feeling of a relaxing coastal vacation, the St. Barts linen robe by Rough Linen is sure to impress with its breathable and surprisingly light fabric.

Give your feet the deluxe treatment, too, with slippers worthy of a five-star hotel. The Ritz-Carlton Frette microfiber slippers are a solid option, offering an ultra-soft cushion and embroidered detailing. Turn to the Sunday Slippers by Bombas for a sturdy pair that’s easy to slip on and feels like a cloud, thanks to the memory foam footbed.

#3142. For America’s Rarest Wildlife, Survival May Hinge on a Single Word

A red-cockaded woodpecker. The species lives in the hollows of longleaf pines in the southeastern U.S. Mac Stone

For 50 years, the Endangered Species Act has defined “harm” as not just injury to individuals but to the habitat on which they depend. By narrowing that definition, the Trump administration is leaving hundreds of species at risk of extinction.

By hatching a new definition of the word “harm,” the Trump administration has effectively killed the Endangered Species Act (ESA).

For 50 years, the legal definition of “harm” has included the destruction of habitat that sustains threatened and endangered species. But a new rule, finalized July 14, redefines harm as only physical damage to individuals. So, provided individuals of a listed species are not directly injured or killed by humans, it’s perfectly okay to destroy all habitat required for their survival, thereby rendering them extinct. “Common sense” is how Interior Secretary Doug Burgum describes it.

Wildlife advocacy groups are outraged, saying the rule change “violates the core purpose of the statute and decades of legal precedent” and is “a stake through the heart of the ESA.” Multiple lawsuits to block the rule are underway. Unless they succeed, the new harm rule will go into effect on September 14.

Under its 50-year-old definition of “harm,” the ESA has prevented 99 percent of listed species from going extinct. That’s because federal agencies were legally mandated to protect and restore critical habitat. They didn’t have a choice. If the new harm rule stands, no such mandate will exist.

Among the horrific damage to wildlife will be the extinction of arguably America’s most endangered mammal, the black-footed ferret. The salvation of the species, presumed extinct before its rediscovery in 1981, has been one of the ESA’s great success stories.

In 1986, just 18 black-footed ferrets remained on the planet, six of which were kept at the Wyoming Game and Fish Department’s Sybille wildlife research facility. Black-footed ferrets are susceptible to human diseases, so when I visited the facility in March of that year, state veterinarian Tom Thorne and I rinsed our feet in antiseptic solution, showered, climbed into blue jumpsuits, and put on rubber sandals and plastic shower caps.

Thorne led me into the ferret room. In a wood-and-wire cage, a young male checked us out, then dove into a drainpipe. I hadn’t expected black-footed ferrets to be so small or so beautiful. “Watch now,” said Thorne. “He’ll pop his head back out and look at us.” He did.

The drainpipe simulated a prairie-dog burrow. Black-footed ferrets prey almost exclusively on prairie dogs and depend on their burrows for shelter — no prairie dogs, no black-footed ferrets. Mandated by the ESA, federal wildlife managers and partners protected and restored prairie-dog towns in some of the best potential ferret habitat across the West. Today there are an estimated 500 black-footed ferrets in the wild, while about 280 others are being held in captive breeding facilities.

A black-footed ferret that was reintroduced near Meeteetse, Wyoming. Alex Milan Tracy / Sipa via AP Images

Under the new definition of harm, it will be legal for ranchers and county, state, and federal agencies to poison prairie dogs in black-footed ferret habitat and for developers to build houses and shopping malls on top of it. The species won’t have a chance.

The new harm rule is also a certain death sentence for the northern spotted owl, which depends on canopy-dense, multi-layered old-growth forest in the Pacific Northwest. The owl is listed as threatened, though an upgrade to endangered status is warranted, according to the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service.

Logging fragments old-growth forests, destroying canopy travel corridors used by the owl’s prey, such as northern flying squirrels and red tree voles. The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service has designated 9.4 million acres as critical habitat for the northern spotted owl. Under the traditional definition of harm, logging northern spotted owl habitat was illegal unless timber companies obtained special permits and submitted plans to mitigate the loss of spotted owls. With the rule change, all that habitat is open for business.

Another spectacular ESA success story is the salvation of red-cockaded woodpeckers. Without the law, the species would now be extinct.

On a crisp day in October 1995, Jay Carter and Kerry Brust, researchers from North Carolina State University, led me to a tall longleaf pine in the Sandhills area of North Carolina. At twilight, an endangered red-cockaded woodpecker dipped from a high bough, emitted a “roosting chuckle” and entered a hole above a long, white sap stain. No other North American woodpecker excavates nest holes exclusively in living trees. The species depends on sticky pine sap to repel predators.

Grim dilemma: Should we kill one owl species to save another? Read more.

Brust pressed a long-handled net over the hole while Carter scratched the bark with a stick to imitate the sound of predator claws. The bird shot into the fine mesh, then shrieked as Brust folded his fingers around it. Carter replaced the red and blue leg bands that identified the bird.

Brust and Carter were part of a multi-agency partnership desperately trying to prevent impending red-cockaded woodpecker extinction. The work was not going well. The longleaf pines these birds need for nesting evolved with wildfire, which opens the cones so seeds can disperse. Historically, fires have also kept understory brush from reaching a height that would allow predators to climb to woodpecker nests. The prevention of wildfires did away with that protection.

A northern spotted owl in Shasta–Trinity National Forest in California. Polinova via Wikipedia

Well before there was an ESA, logging eradicated longleaf pines, while wildfire prevention prohibited their regeneration, destroying most of the woodpecker’s habitat. The original longleaf forest covered at least 92 million acres of the Southeastern United States, sustaining an estimated 3 million red-cockaded woodpeckers. By 1995, only about 3 million acres of longleaf forest survived, and the woodpecker population was down to fewer than 15,000 birds.

What’s more, the ESA was under attack by property-rights activists, represented and incited by Congressmen Don Young (R-Alaska) and Richard Pombo (R-Calif.), who sponsored legislation that would have legalized harm to habitat, just as the Trump administration has done. And, fearing that the ESA would prevent them from logging woodpecker habitat, landowners were clearcutting longleaf pines before their holdings could be surveyed for woodpeckers.

Enter Bruce Babbitt, President Clinton’s Interior Secretary, who saved the ESA with “Safe Harbor” agreements, by which landowners pledged to create new red-cockaded woodpecker habitat by administering prescribed burns to open cones and release seeds, pruning to keep understories low, allowing longleaf pines to mature, and installing artificial nest cavities in the trees. In return, the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service allowed the landowners to harvest the longleaf pines on that new habitat, provided they gave the agency time to capture and relocate any nesting birds to other pine forests.

Saving the species from imminent extinction were 459 Safe Harbor agreements across 2.5 million acres. The red-cockaded woodpecker population increased by 430 percent from the late 1970s to 2024 — the year they were downlisted from endangered to threatened. Had the Trump administration’s redefinition of harm been in place, there would have been no ESA habitat mandate. And with no constraints on logging by landowners, and no motivation for them to create new habitat, Safe Harbor wouldn’t have worked.

Black-footed ferrets, northern spotted owls, and red-cockaded woodpeckers are just three of hundreds of ESA-listed species certain to be ushered into oblivion by the Trump administration’s nonsensical harm rule.

Supporters of the new harm rule note that it will not apply to land managed by the National Park Service. But many listed species on that land rely on habitat in adjacent buffer zones where the new rule will be in effect. For example, grizzly bears, Canada lynx, and wolverines in Yellowstone National Park all inhabit adjacent national forests in the Greater Yellowstone Ecosystem. And Florida panthers establish large territories extending far beyond the boundaries of Everglades National Park and Big Cypress National Preserve. Removing protection for all such non-park habitat would diminish populations within National Park Service land and lead to isolation and inbreeding.

As jaguars recover, will the border wall block their U.S. return? Read more.

What do Americans think about the new harm rule? The answer is evident in the almost 360,000 public comments filed with the Trump administration. While the administration declines to list the number or proportion of negative responses, an analysis by Earthjustice reveals that about 99 percent of the comments were opposed. Under the Administrative Procedure Act, federal agencies must consider public commentary and provide a rational, evidence-based explanation for their final rule. The Trump administration routinely ignores this directive.

Unless plaintiffs succeed in their lawsuit to block the administration’s redefinition of “harm,” the most successful fish and wildlife protection and recovery law ever enacted will essentially cease to exist.

Ted Williams is a contributing writer for Yale Environment 360. He writes about fish and wildlife for national publications. More about Ted Williams →

Thereâs a question that seems to come up fairly often in conversations about âextraâ belongings that won’t be needed while someone travels long-term. Especially with those who have not yet fully questioned how much less they could live with.

Regardless of how far you think you might go on minimizing, itâs a question I never want you to consider answering in the affirmative.Â

The question? “Canât I just get a long-term storage unit for my stuff for a while?â

I’ve discussed this with many location-independent and nomadic friends that did use long-term storage facilities while traveling long-term, and their response is almost always the same:

“What a waste of money. I wish I had gotten rid of it all sooner.”

Itâs a valid question. Itâs also a very American question. Actually, it’s a question almost completely relegated to US citizens. Recent studies show that the United States has amassed almost 90% of the self-storage facilities when compared to the rest of the world!

The long-term self-storage industry in the United States is a nearly $40 billion a year industry. And whereas the industry was spending around $1 billion a year in 2015 to build new consumer storage spaces, they had increased four-fold to nearly $4 billion a year. (References later in this article.)

I wonât mince words. Paying to store excess personal belongings not destined for use at any time in the extremely near future is a wildly wasteful way to spend your hard-earned money!

Iâm guessing you might know where Iâm going with this. The US dollar can go so much further in places outside of the US. So letâs look at some of these costs on a yearly basis, since most of those long-term storage companies make you sign a year contract.

One of the things you’ll also want to consider if you’re planning to store belongings to travel is the depreciation of value of the things you’re storing. This won’t be the case for everything, but higher-priced tech like stereos, computers, and appliances will continue to be worth less month-by-month while they sit in storage.

Example: If you could sell a big ticket item for $500 but instead choose to store it for a year, it may only be worth $350-400 by the time you get it out of storage. So not only have you spent money to store it…you’ve lost money on your return!

Combine the potential depreciation of 4-5 expensive items like this and you could be losing a lot more than you thought possible at the beginning of this thought process! I’m hyper-sensitive about anything that has a finance fee or “opportunity cost” that seems overly detrimental to my bottom line in the long run. This definitely seems to fall into that category. Watch out!

As you can see, $720-$2700 per year spent on long-term storageâstoring items that arenât getting much useâcould be much better spent living for months in different nomad-friendly cities around the world. Places where the US currency is very strong.

Combine extra losses in depreciation of items you could have potentially soldâand invested the money and maybe made a few percentâand there’s more financial carnage.

I guarantee you have a friend or family member that will let you store a good sized chunk of stuff for a few months while you travel so you can avoid falling into the long-term storage unit money trap.

Let a friend or family member “borrow” your things indefinitely, donate them to a good cause, or just sell it all to fund your next adventure!

I used to have a cello. I enjoyed playing it with musician friends of mine in jam sessions, just for fun. I always thought about taking more lessons and getting better at it. When I went nomadic, I lent it “indefinitely” to a friend of mine who wanted to learn the cello. She ended up never doing much with it either, but one of her friends expressed interest in buying it, so I sold it to the friend of a friend. I doubt I’ll take up the cello again, but if I do I will just buy another one, which will be cheaper than it would have cost to put it in climate-controlled storage for years on end.

Yep, this is what I find happens to a lot of new travelers; they save things “just in case” but just end up selling or giving away later. Early storage might be necessary, but too long and it’s a lot of lost loot! Cheers Lily!
