#3145. ‘The tech bro fight club is real!’ The titans of AI get the Succession treatment in a wild new TV show

The Audacity is a fearless satire taking on Silicon Valley’s ‘egomaniac weirdos’. Its creator and stars talk ayahuasca ceremonies, mining kids for data – and punching CEOs

Showrunner Jonathan Glatzer wanted the tagline of his latest TV creation to be: “We’re so fucked.” He laughs. “Sadly it got rejected by the network. Hey, maybe I can use it for season two!” The show in question is The Audacity, his darkly comic drama about AI tech titans. Instead, it went with the more PG tagline “Move fast and break people” – a twist on “Move fast and break things”, the Facebook motto coined by Mark Zuckerberg. The Audacity promises to do for tech bros what Succession did for legacy media. Considering Glatzer is a former writer on Succession, that is no empty threat.

This acerbic and all-too-timely Silicon Valley satire follows CEO Duncan Park (played by Billy Magnussen), the obscenely wealthy founder of a data analytics company. He’s a self-proclaimed “inventor of the future” who also happens to be a morally bankrupt man-baby. “Duncan is worth about half a billion,” says Glatzer. “Naturally, that’s not enough. He desperately wants to be a billionaire and ascend to what we call ‘God Mountain’. But he’s no God.”

Like Succession, The Audacity’s scripts are liberally spiced with baroque swearing. Park boasts about keeping investors “moist” and “Viagra-ing the share price to keep it up”. Magnussen delivers such juicy dialogue with relish. “Jonathan has a way of painting a picture with words while also getting nasty with it,” he grins. “My favourite line in season two is: ‘I pulled it out of my ass and it came out clean.’ You’ll have to wait and see what it refers to.” It’s a measure of The Audacity’s whip-smart relevance that the second season was commissioned before the first had even aired.

“Unquestionably, there’s a tech bro mentality that is sexualised,” says Glatzer. “Silicon Valley is such a male-dominated culture. It’s also very white or south Asian. When I first came here, I didn’t see a person of colour for two weeks. That struck me as very odd in a major US city. Hiring practices are dictated by algorithms. The algorithms say that if you went to these universities, you’re our man. If you came from someplace else, and are maybe not a man, the algorithm says you might be problematic. And you could end up writing a book about us.”

The Audacity pulls no punches on how the tech sector is wrecking the planet. “It sure is,” says Glatzer. “Generative AI is environmentally ruinous. Datacentres are tremendous suckers of energy. Worryingly, it doesn’t take too many rampantly ambitious entrepreneurs to fuck up an entire planet for the rest of us.” When Park is warned about the huge carbon footprint of his “algo”, he shrugs: “I’ll sponsor a penguin.”

Zach Galifianakis plays billionaire genius Carl Bardolph, who “basically created spam”. He’s rich enough to retire but becomes a disruptor out of boredom. “I’ve always been very concerned about the people running tech,” says Galifianakis. “They have math minds, but that sometimes comes with a lack of empathy. About 12 nerds are controlling our lives and designing our futures. And they all tend to have the same profile. Most come from Stanford University. I don’t know what they’re teaching there.” He laughs. “It’s weird and kind of evil.”

Did Galifianakis base his portrayal on anyone specific? “He’s pulled from one dude I’ve watched over the years. I won’t give his name but he’s a real piece of shit. A major jerk. The guys running Silicon Valley are damaged. Carl has a giant hole in his heart and a ton of anger because of his dad. I’ve seen a lot of men like that in Hollywood, so I drew on them too. Most of the puppet masters are egomaniac weirdos and I wouldn’t mind saying that to their faces. Zuckerberg has something missing. Elon Musk is bizarre. They’re little boys. You almost feel sorry for them.”

“Duncan also deals with daddy issues and a lack of love,” says Magnussen. “A lot of tech bros are trying to fill voids. The great irony is that the most antisocial people work in social media. Those who don’t know how to be personable are controlling how we communicate. It’s nuts.”

Both actors view the show as a howl of protest. “I’ve done some cool shit in my career,” says Magnussen. “I’ve been in Bond [he played a CIA agent in No Time to Die]. I’ve been in fun, escapist movies. But this feels different. It’s a call to arms. People need to wake up to the audacious behaviour of this handful of people. Their decisions affect 8.3 billion people. That’s scary, man. I just hope our show opens some conversations. We’ve got to start making a difference.”

Galifianakis agrees. “It’s a warning. I’ve never felt this way before with my work – come on, I was in a movie with a baby wearing sunglasses [The Hangover] – but this show’s important. This industry has no guard rails. You can’t sue them, you can’t do anything, so of course they’re going to take advantage of the population. That’s exactly what they’ve done. The destructive environmental impact alone is enough reason to take to the streets and protest. Sure, there’s all this darkness online but also, we’re going to take your water and electricity from you, too.”

Park blackmails his therapist – sorry, “performance psychologist” (played by Barry’s Sarah Goldberg) – into giving him insider-trading tips from her other powerful clients. The pair are soon engulfed in a scandal over fraudulent stock pumping. It’s a familiar-sounding storyline. “Elon got into trouble for market manipulation,” says Glatzer. “It’s like Duncan says in the show: ‘It’s not a felony in Silicon Valley, it’s good hustle.’ That’s the mentality here. Laws are for morons, not us.”

Real world echoes are everywhere in The Audacity. The search engine of choice is called Spookle (slogan: “You Agreed to This”). A corporation called Cupertino is a thinly veiled reference to Apple, while its mythical boss “Big Tim” nods to Tim Cook. Are any of Glatzer’s characters directly based on real-life figures? “The truth is, I don’t find any of them worthy of a three-dimensional character that could go the distance of multiple seasons,” says Glatzer. “They’re interesting enough for a film like The Social Reckoning but not much more.”

Indeed, Magnussen can currently be seen in The Social Reckoning as a Meta spin doctor. Is this his tech bro era? “AI is the most pressing issue of our time,” he says. “It affects our planet, our politics, our mental health, everything. Technology can do wonderful things but once it starts dividing us or swaying elections, it’s deeply damaging.”

“I don’t understand why people get on board with social media,” sighs Galifianakis. “We fed these machines for years. Now these machines are going to find us. I don’t believe the promises of AI because I don’t trust the people doing it.”

The Audacity’s protagonist has been described as “highly punchable”. Magnussen concurs: “You’ve seen these guys in front of Congress. They’re pretty fucking punchable.” An early episode includes a sequence at a Palo Alto fight club, where nerds whack each other with keyboards and strangle each other with cables. “That’s real!” laughs Magnussen. “The tech bro fight club is an actual thing.”

Despite the plentiful real-world parallels, Glatzer has never met a tech mogul. “That’s fine by me,” he says. “I don’t imagine the conversation would be anything other than stage-managed and dishonest. They have people workshopping their handshake and smile, so what am I going to get from that? But I have talked to a number of people who work in the tech world. The first thing they say is: ‘I’m not like that. That’s those other guys. I’m one of the good ones.’”

“Me too,” says Magnussen. “The response I get is: ‘I know that guy!’ It’s always someone else. You want to go: ‘No, it’s you. You’re that guy.’”

In America’s richest enclave, hi-tech goes hand-in-hand with hippy-dippy. The Audacity lampoons heath fads like ayahuasca ceremonies, microdosing and bio-hacking. “Tech titans feel as if they’ve got the golden ticket,” says Glatzer. “They want their prime to last as long as possible, perhaps for ever. Maybe the singularity comes when they upload themselves into an AI consciousness and become gods. I don’t have proof of that being the reason why so many datacentres are being built by these guys – but I don’t think it’s uninvolved.”

“There’s no limit to these people’s overreach,” agrees Magnussen. “They truly believe their life is more valuable than anyone else’s.”

The show also skewers the hypocrisy of execs who ban their own offspring from smartphones, reasoning that “arms dealers don’t give their kids landmines”. “It’s like how a lot of Hollywood don’t have TVs in their houses,” says Galifianakis. “Because they know the poison they’re selling.”

“They know the dangers,” nods Glatzer. “It’s a classic case of capitalism gone haywire. I don’t think they’d live next door to a datacentre either. None of them do what they’re telling us to do. It used to be that people in public life had to practise what they preached. That has quietly gone away. US congresspeople invest in the same tech companies they’re supposedly regulating. Look at what Trump’s doing. It’s a kleptocracy.”

He’s equally concerned by “screenification” in schools. “The amount of information gathered on our kids is frightening,” says Glatzer. “Shopping habits, medical history, exam grades. When schools get given tablets in the States, the law says it’s OK for the provider to track that student. We’re handing our kids these screens with the patina of being an educational tool but they are data-collecting devices. Just like everything is.”

“I can’t believe how much information people give up,” says Galifianakis. “If you go to LA, there’s all these cars that say Google on the side, taking pictures of everything. If those cars said US Government, people would lose their minds. But because it’s a corporation, we all sit back and go: ‘I guess this is the future.’ I think people are starting to wake up but maybe it’s too late.” He laughs. “What do I know? I’m just a dumb actor trying to alarm people. But our children are the new oil. They’re being mined for data.”

“I’m most scared for the kids,” agrees Magnussen. “They’ve been digital since they were born. They’re being manipulated without even knowing.”

In The Audacity, even seemingly benign tech is sinister. Eccentric inventor Martin Phister (The Big Bang Theory’s Simon Helberg) is developing an empathetic bot to be a digital “friend” for alienated teens. US viewers have likened him to OpenAI’s Sam Altman, who initially presented as a sympathetic mogul.

“Martin’s trying to do something good,” says Glatzer. “You later learn that he’s compensating for something very bad. Carl has a speech about his generation coming to a crossroads: ‘Leave a legacy of good or go full Dr Evil.’ Most choose the latter. They are drawn to doing something positive but they are Hungry Hungry Hippos. They can’t resist the allure of a good deal.”

“Carl knew the internet could have been a good thing for society if we’d done it right,” adds Galifianakis. “But greed and old-fashioned American competition, the need to win, became more important than human beings.”

Geeky idealism is long gone. “It began as a desire to change the world for the better,” says Glatzer. “A bunch of nerdy engineers wore hoodies rather than suits and put ‘Do No Evil’ slogans on their office walls. That’s all in the rear-view mirror.”

Glatzer freely admits that his work on Succession informed The Audacity. “There’s no way that it didn’t. But that was about institutional, dynastic wealth, whereas our characters are self-made. They are Logan Roy as a young man, if he had decided to be a tech guy rather than a media baron. What I certainly gleaned from Jesse Armstrong was finding a tone that enables you to give the establishment some real gut punches. Sarcasm might be the protest of the weak, but that’s all we’ve got. Speaking as a weakling.”

The invasive cyber-surveillance and data-mining in The Audacity are deeply chilling. “We consulted experts but all the information is out there,” says Glatzer. “The Guardian and New York Times do some terrific reporting on AI. It always feels like the journalists secretly want to headline their articles: ‘Wake the fuck up! This is happening!’”

#3146. As a young man, magazines were my window to the world. That wonder will be lost when all the shops have gone | John Harris

The closure of former WH Smith stores around the UK is sobering on many levels. The publications they sold were there to be perused, to broaden horizons

Towards the end of the 1970s, the brief but spectacular career of those punk rockers and folk devils the Sex Pistols was followed by The Great Rock’n’Roll Swindle, a rather tawdry drama-documentary. It recast their rise and fall as a huge scam, summed up by a slogan on a T-shirt worn by their former manager Malcolm McLaren: “Cash from Chaos”.

Back then, that motto was a neat representation of his personal brand of cultural mischief. But the culture always takes unexpected twists and turns – and 50 years on, an almost-identical maxim seems to be defining the fate of an increasing chunk of the British retail economy. While Andy Burnham pledges to restore life to town centres, a private equity outfit is busy buying up national retail chains and then shutting down scores of their outlets, while seemingly operating in line with its boss’s most celebrated quotation: “Where there’s chaos, there’s opportunity.”

Those words came from Jamie Constable, the owner of a set of businesses that includes Modella Capital, which specialises in “distressed investing” and “aggressive restructuring”. In 2024, Modella bought the craft supplies chain Hobbycraft – insisting on its “real commitment to bricks-and-mortar retail”, but then shutting 18 shops and triggering 600 job losses, while leaving redundancy costs to the taxpayer. Months later, it acquired the discount outlets branded as The Original Factory Shop, only for the entire chain to collapse. But its most ambitious move was its recent purchase of the 480 high-street shops that previously traded as WH Smith, rebranded by Modella as TG Jones (a completely made-up name, which it said would “keep that sense of a family business”), and the rapidly announced closure of up to 150 of them.

In September, 19 branches breathed their last, in such varied locations as Bath, Southend, Cardigan, Redhill and the Essex new town of Basildon. This month, the shutters are permanently falling in – among 23 places – Pinner, Truro, Inverness and Leek in Staffordshire. Thanks to their shortsighted folding into previous WH Smith premises, the closure plan will inevitably mean the loss of many local post offices: by early 2027, 70 will have gone. It will inevitably accelerate the decline of printed newspapers. But for me and many others, the key tragedy in this story is all about magazines, those gloriously tactile creations that played a crucial role in making many of us who we are.

I say that as someone who fell in love with this form of publishing as a child, via a weekly educational magazine called Look and Learn – stuffed, as I recall, with comic-strip versions of Dickens stories, and stuff about space exploration. By my early teens, I was an avid reader of Sounds, the Melody Maker and NME, the so-called weekly music papers that were really magazines whose ink came off on your fingers. Much to my amazement, between my 19th and 26th birthdays, I ended up successively working for all three – until, in 1995, I joined the now defunct company Emap Metro, which set enormous store by its mastery of glossy fortnightlies and monthlies.

Some of these names may mean precious little to younger readers, but spark a Proustian rush among older ones: Q, Smash Hits, Select (which I edited for three years), Sky, the metal and hard rock title Kerrang!, Big, Neon, FHM. Most have now closed, but three of the old stable are still standing, under different ownership: Kerrang!, which mostly lives online; the film bible Empire; and Mojo, the lovingly produced music magazine that I am thrilled to still write for.

Thirty years ago, we all knew that there was no better medium for in-depth interviews, or high-end photography. The management constantly asked us who our intended reader was, and what the future was likely to entail. Even the smallest cover-lines were pored over, as if the mere mention of Placebo or DJ Shadow might attract scores of newbies. And though a lot of us didn’t listen, we were told to never, ever put our magazines in sealed plastic bags (which, in line with a longstanding convention, would contain “free gifts”), for fear of losing the kind of punter who liked to browse before they bought.

And therein lies the TG Jones tragedy. The business has rightly acquired an utterly grim reputation, but is – or was – one reliable place to still find shelves full of printed content worthy of endless curiosity. And now? The culturally omnivorous twentysomething in Rickmansworth will no longer chance upon the latest edition of the cutting-edge music monthly the Wire, and thereby be inducted into a world of esoteric electronica and contemporary avant-garde jazz. The student-age customer in Ormskirk tiring of production-line pop may know nothing of the more substantial music covered by Mojo’s excellent competitor Uncut.

Would-be radicals will be unable to browse the printed iteration of Tribune; film freaks may be completely unaware of the in-depth, art-house wonders of Sight and Sound. Publishers will fall over themselves to alert us to subscription offers, but for a lot of people, they will be prohibitively expensive.

As George Orwell once put it, the UK is “a nation of stamp-collectors, pigeon-fanciers, amateur carpenters, coupon-snippers, darts-players, crossword-puzzle fans”. Products that reflect that enduring aspect of our national character are still at the core of magazine publishing, and why some titles still sell enviably: at the last count, for example, BBC Gardeners’ World sold a monthly average of 164,807 copies, while Country Living shifted 107,596. The broad women’s and fashion sector is also still here. It isn’t all over, by any means.

Guardian columnists and writers on what they’ve been debating, thinking about, reading, and more

Still, one can easily imagine a Mark Zuckerberg-esque voice scoffing at all this as so much stone-age nonsense, and claiming that what printed magazines deliver can easily be either replicated or superseded on the internet. Experience often suggests otherwise: witness the fate of the once-independent online music magazine Pitchfork, bought out by the corporate publisher Condé Nast in 2015 and bizarrely folded into the workings of the men’s title GQ nine years later: a classic case of what we now know as enshittification. But there is an even bigger point here about how we now engage with the things we like and love, summarised in a forthcoming book titled Circulation: How Culture Moves in the Age of Algorithm by the Nashville-based US music insider David Macias, with a foreword written by Dan Clancy, the CEO of the livestreaming platform Twitch.

His argument is about music, but its logic applies across the cultural, recreational and sporting board. “The algorithm is great at finding me new music in the limited style I listen to,” he writes. “But it is really bad at showing me something new and different. My listening has become homogeneous, even though I can pick any album ever created.” This is the tedious prospect that the demise of the magazine will only accelerate – so after the high-street chaos has died down, we could be faced with an awful silence, broken only by the leakage from headphones playing the same things over and over again. And to speak the language of the insatiable capitalist, where will anyone find money in that?

#3147. Easy Turkey STEM Challenges

It’s finally December, the elves are up to their usual mischief, presents are being wrapped, and people are looking forward to plenty of festive fun with family and friends. My Turkey STEM Challenges are great for encouraging hands-on learning before or during the Christmas festivities.

Learn about strong shapes and stable structures with a Turkey Tower.

Find out why calcium is so important by making bendy turkey bones.

Build a Turkey Trap with items from your recycling bin.

My FREE turkey challenges and worksheets are great for adding a little structure to the investigations.

We used cocktail sticks (toothpicks) and marshmallows to build our tower. We found that creating triangle shapes in the structure made it much stronger.

Plasticine or some types of candy work well if you want an alternative to marshmallows.

Learn more about strong shapes with my paper column activity.

Bones are strong because they contain lots of calcium carbonate. My bendy bones STEM challenge uses vinegar to remove calcium from a turkey bone, demonstrating how bones lose strength when they lose calcium.

If you leave a cooked turkey bone in vinegar for about 48 hours, you’ll find it becomes so soft you can bend it.

The idea behind this activity is to build a trap to fit a small turkey toy or figure.

For this challenge, you need to build a boat shape using foil that can hold some gravy ( use water if you don’t want to waste actual gravy ) without sinking.

Do you have more Turkey STEM Challenges we can try?

#3149. Vorgezogene Parlamentswahlen in Serbien: Machtwechsel oder weiter mit Aleksandar Vucic?

Kommt es zum Showdown gegen den langjährigen Machthaber und Ex-Präsidenten Staatschef Aleksandar Vucic? Bei den vorgezogenen Parlamentswahlen in Serbien am Sonntag geht es um einen politischen Richtungswechsel im Land.

Kurz vor den Wahlen erfasste Belgrad am Donnerstag vergangener Woche eine Welle der Hoffnung. Rund 200.000 Menschen waren auf dem Slavija-Platz im Zentrum von Belgrad zusammengekommen, um ihre Unterstützung für die Studentenliste Serbien zu demonstrieren.

Und um gegen die Regierungspartei von Aleksandar Vucic zu protestieren. Das oppositionelle Bündnis umfasst 250 Kandidatinnen und Kandidaten und fordert bei den Parlamentswahlen am Sonntag die Macht des Populisten Vucic heraus.

"Wir sind quer durch Serbien gelaufen, von einem Ende des Landes zum anderen, haben die Menschen in Dörfern und Stadtvierteln besucht, Ihnen in die Augen geschaut und erkannt, dass wir auf Sie zählen können", rief der Biologiestudent Arsa Stojanović bei der Abschlusskundgebung in die Menge.

"Leute, es riecht nach Freiheit", fügte Aleksandar Čupić, Professor für Verkehrswesen und Kandidat auf der Studentenliste, hinzu.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Nach zwei Jahre Protesten steuert Serbien auf eine Wahl zu, die sich deutlich von allen anderen Urnengängen unterscheidet, seit die Serbische Fortschrittspartei (SNS) vor 14 Jahren erstmals an die Macht kam.

Zum ersten Mal seit Jahren muss die Regierungspartei um ihre Macht bangen. Denn die Studentenbewegung hat die weitverbreitete Unzufriedenheit der Bevölkerung in einen erfolgreichen Wahlkampf verwandelt.

Ausgelöst wurden die Proteste durch den Einsturz eines Betondachs am Bahnhof von Novi Sad im November 2024. Dabei kamen 16 Menschen ums Leben.

"Dies sind die ersten Wahlen seit Vucics Machtantritt im Jahr 2012, die wirklich umkämpft und deren Ausgang ungewiss ist", erklärt Politikwissenschaftler Dušan Spasojević gegenüber DW.

"Die Studentenbewegung hat bei den Bürgern Hoffnungen geweckt und erhebliche Unterstützung gewonnen. Umfragen legen nahe, dass sie sogar mehr als 40 Prozent der Stimmen gewinnen könnte."

Die Studentenliste präsentiert die Wahl als Gelegenheit, Korruption zu bekämpfen und fordert, Verantwortliche für die Katastrophe von Novi Sad zu benennen.

Die Regierungspartei SNS hingegen präsentiert sich als Garant für Kontinuität. Sie hielt, ebenfalls in Belgrad, nur wenige Kilometer von der Studentenbewegung, ihre eigene Abschlusskundgebung ab.

Die Botschaft war vertraut: Eine Stimme für die SNS bedeute Stabilität, Kontinuität und weiteren Wohlstand, während ein Regierungswechsel das Risiko berge, das Land ins Chaos zu stürzen.

"Sie haben versucht, unser Land auf ausländische Anweisung zu zerstören, und deshalb ist dies die härteste Schlacht", so Vucic zu seinen Anhängern.

Aufgrund der vorgezogenen Präsidentschaftswahlentrat Vucic am 27. September 2026 von seinem Amt zurück, um am 25. Oktober 2026 erneut als Spitzenkandidat der Fortschrittspartei anzutreten - für das Amt des Ministerpräsidenten.

Als Staatspräsident wäre seine Amtszeit am 31. Mai 2027 beendet gewesen. Eine dritte Amtszeit lässt die Verfassung nicht zu.

Der Wahlkampf war von Gewalt überschattet. Bis zur letzten Wahlkampfwoche hatte die unabhängige Wahlbeobachtungsorganisation CRTA116 Vorfälle mit körperlicher Gewalt oder verbalen Drohungen registriert. Dazu gehörten tätliche Auseinandersetzungen, Angriffe auf Wahlkampfhelfer und Versuche, Wahlkampfplakate oder Banner zu entfernen.

CRTA berichtete außerdem, dass Oppositionsaktivisten, insbesondere solche mit Verbindungen zur Studentenliste, Überwachung und Polizeibefragungen ausgesetzt waren. In einigen Fällen, so die Organisation, habe die Polizei nur zögerlich Oppositionskandidaten und deren Unterstützer geschützt.

Laut CRTA war der Wahlkampf in Serbien weder frei noch fair. So gebe es "ein deutliches Ungleichgewicht in der Medienberichterstattung": Fast drei Viertel der Sendezeit für politische Akteure im nationalen Fernsehen entfielen auf Regierungsvertreter, während mehr als 80 Prozent der Berichterstattung über die Studentenliste negativ gewesen seien.

Vor der Wahl veröffentlichte das Balkan Investigative Reporting Network (BIRN)die Aussage eines ehemaligen SNS-Mitglieds, das schilderte, wie Beschäftigte des öffentlichen Dienstes unter Druck gesetzt worden seien, Stimmen für die Partei zu gewinnen.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Die DW erfuhr zudem aus anonymen Quellen, dass Wählern zwischen 30 und 150 Euro angeboten worden seien, damit sie für die SNS stimmten. Ganzen Familien seien Berichten zufolge sogar Urlaubsreisen versprochen worden, wenn alle Familienmitglieder ihre Stimmen der Regierungspartei gäben.

Viele Menschen befürchten nach der Wahl eine Phase politischer Unsicherheit. Besonders die Wahlnacht selbst gilt als Anlass zu großer Sorge. Die Bewegung hat angekündigt, jede einzelne Stimme zu verteidigen.

Seit Wochen warnen die serbischen Behörden davor, dass Studenten mit angeblicher Unterstützung ausländischer Geheimdienste Gewalt auf den Straßen vorbereiteten. In den letzten Tagen des Wahlkampfs wurden mehrere Personen festgenommen, die laut Staatsanwaltschaft verdächtigt werden, Störungen in der Wahlnacht geplant zu haben.

Das günstigste Szenario wäre laut Politikwissenschaftler Dušan Spasojević ein klarer Sieg eines der beiden politischen Lager, der kaum Raum für Anfechtungen lassen würde.

"Die Umfragen deuten jedoch darauf hin, dass der Abstand knapp sein wird, und wir werden wahrscheinlich in eine Art Nachwahlkrise eintreten", prophezeit er.

#3150. Who Gets to Decide the Fate of a Pristine Alaskan Rainforest?

In “Land Less” — the Third-Place Winner of the 2026 Yale Environment 360 Film Contest — filmmaker Alexi Liotti follows the activists fighting a bill that would transfer thousands of acres of Alaskan rainforest to five Native corporations, opening the land to logging.

More than half a century ago, the U.S. Congress transferred 44 million acres of public lands in Alaska to for-profit Native corporations, which have been free to exploit those lands, including by logging them. Last year, the state’s congressional delegation introduced a new bill, the Alaska Native Landless Equity Act, which would transfer an additional 115,000 acres from the Tongass National Forest — the world’s largest temperate rainforest — to five newly formed Native corporations. Those lands contain 80,000 acres of ancient forest, including 60,000 roadless acres. Should the bill pass, many environmental activists fear much of that land will be clearcut.

Last summer, British Columbia-based filmmaker Alexi Liotti followed one of those activists, Joshua Wright, as he traveled through southeast Alaska, using satellite imagery to document pristine and highly biodiverse areas at risk of being logged under the Landless Act. In Juneau, Wright met with Native Tlingit matriarch and Tongass defender Wanda Culp, who decried the proposed legislation and likened the logging of old-growth forest to “slicing up our culture.”

As Liotti filmed, he says his focus shifted from the Landless Act itself to questioning who gets to take responsibility for and ultimately decide the future of public lands. “I hope people come away seeing places like the Tongass not as lines on a map,” he says, “but as living landscapes whose future depends on the choices we make today.”

About the Filmmaker: Alexi Liotti is a Canadian documentary filmmaker whose work explores the relationship between people, place, and nature. His debut feature documentary, Rematriation, received the Best Environmental Film award at the Vancouver International Mountain Film Festival. His films have been supported by ORTOVOX, Arc’teryx, The North Face, Red Bull, and the CBC.

About the Contest: Now in its 13th season, the Yale Environment 360 Film Contest honors the year’s best environmental documentary shorts from around the world. This year we received 596 submissions from more than 80 countries across six continents, with the winners selected by the editors of Yale Environment 360.
